WOMEN'S CHAT.

Queen Alexandra's Friends.—The wedding of the Earl of Macclesfield, who has just come of age and Miss Lilian Boyle. will be ono- in which the Queen will be specially interested, for his grendmother, the Countess of Macclesfield, is one of her Majesty's oldest friends. She was appointed a Lady-in-Waiting to Queen Alexandra at the time of her Majesty's marriage in 1863, and is one of the few remaining "grand old ladies" •of the Victorian era. She has nearly reached the great age of eighty-eight, having been born (ate year later than her sister Eleanor, Dechess of Northumberland. Among the -older friends of her Majesty, the first place is filled by the Bore Chariots e Knollys, who has been the Queen's constant companion throughout her wedded life. Miss Knollys has not been in the best of . health lately, but the was happily, able to accompany the Queen for the cruise in the Mediterranean, still main- taining her record of never having been separated from her Majesty for over 45 years. The Feminist Campaign.—The ra'sieg of Joan of Arc to sainthood seems to have insphed the women suffragists with a-desire to emulate the warrior spirit of the Abild of Orleans, who was impersonated by Mies Elsie Howey in the great proct ssion in London, to celebrate the release of Mrs. Pethiek Lawrence hem gaol. Miss Howey, clad in glistening armour with purple, white and green plumes on her helmet, was riding a white steed, and was ft popu'ar feature of the -procession which was half a mile long, and exhibited a large number of bannerettes with , divers inspiriting mottoes. In one way and another, the feminist move-. anent is showing a great deal of life and activity, and the various Secieties and Unions display a remarkable power of organisation. The Women's Suffrage Inter- nathanal Conference, will be attended by a large num- ber of delegates from America end the Colonies., the two principal events being the great meetingsat the Albert Hail on April 27th, and the afternoon rervice at St. Paul's Cathedral on Sunday. May 2nd. Our Newer Was s.—The new banners of the Women's Social and Political Union bears the motto "Strong souls live like fire-hearted suns to spend "their strength." But it is not only to obtain votes th tt women will nowadays do things that in the past would have teen considered unthinkabfe. For instance the residents of Washington have been cemplaining of the dirty and neglected state of the streets. There are- some things that are not better done in America than elsewhere, and the care of the roads and sidewalks is one of them—so nobody was surprised when the authorities took no notice of these protons. What however, did astonish the inhabitants, was the appear- ance the other day of a number of society women in the streets, who, with the necessary implements, set to work cleaning up the main thoroughfares. The Children Act.—There is a good deal of doubt and misunderstending as to the woraing of the Children Act, especially with regard to the pr s nce of young; people on licensed premises. Parliament no doubt had the test intentiene, and the test of reasens, for passing this legislation, but it is causing trouble to reputable ' parents, who, having taken their children for a country outing, have complained of being refused . admittance to wayside inns, where they wanted tea, there being nowhere else in the neighbourhood, wher o this could be obtained. One knows that tie Children Act is , intended only to excluie children from drinking bars ' but at some ants it is stated that Al the rooms are licensed, and childrsn cannot be taken in at all. Nor is it only some of the innkeepers who are disposed to aegard the letter of the law too strictly. The Act does not close railway refreshment rooms to children, • yet, when travelling with young folks at Easter holiday time many parents were afraid to enter the refreshment TOMS, although perhaps the children were tired and thirty after a long railway. journsy. It is , evident that the children Act has raised problems which will affect rr any parents now that the picnic and summer excursion season is corning on. The Old Ds-inning Days.—in the reign of Cnarles 11, it was considered correct not only for every gentleman. but else for every lady of fashion to drink to a stale of intoxication, and it is woneerfnl how the children ever grew up at 81ln/sec4ng that they too regularly hal their beer. Sir John Hawkin i s n his " History of 3.latsic " gives the nursery menu of the Percy family in the 16th century. The food which at ordinary times consisted of a liberal supply of bread, butter, fish, mutton bones, and so one, varied at different seasons, but all the year round eaeh of the two children, a boy and a girl, had their quart of bees for breakfast. Ale drinking too, figured largely in the days' enjoyment when young maidens went cote ting,and Queen Elizabeth is said to have been of opinion that all bser was gooi, only some hinds were better than others. It- would have been a difficult joh to have enforced the Childrens' Act in then-, days. A Great Literary LSK..—The death .of Mr. Mari, n ! Crawford is a sad blow to his conntlers admirers, and their loss will come keenly home'when they take up,l what alas, must be his last \wee. " The White Sister' —which in the cou:se of a week or so w'll be published by Messrs. Macmillan. It is twenty-seven years since Mr. Crawferd's first took " Mr. Isaacs "took the world by storm, and this was followed by -" Dr. Ciand:tm,". 4` A Roman Singer," " Sant Ileris," " A Cigarette Maker's Romance." etc., etc. In all Mr. Marion Craw- ford has given us some forty delightful noYels, all fa.scinat ins, the majority instructive, many taking us to the Italy the late, much-lamented an hor loved so well. A Delightful Revival—Our old feulardis with us again, and horn Paris comes the prediction that we ahall take itaeriously during the coming summer. The favourite colour combine: i& n is wavy and white, and the leading designs the spots end the sprig. A more accommodating fabric than foulard it would be difficult to discover, as it is possible to fashion it into a simple country morning gown, or the smartest afternoon toilette. Moreover it wears excellently, and is suitable for old and young alike. , Beauty and Fashions at the Dalmeny-Grosvenor Wedding.— ['he very- tight sleeve was greatly in evidence at the Dalmeny-Gioeveror siediing, many specimens being fastened from shoulder to wrist by means of tiny bootee and thread eyes, suclzeorocedure ensuring a. perfect fit. Htadgran generally spotting, wes extreme, and perhaps more weird and curious, t nt. beaming, but the gowns and avrapsesta a whole, were superb. Rarely, too' has such an assembly of fine handsome women and girls been seen, and the guest who, during the reception -was heard to remark that the function might well have been styled a "beauty show "was speak;ng truly, and with a fins discrimination. New Jewellery.—A craze for creepy, crawly jewellery has susan again, and the most realistic spiders, beetles and snakes are being sought after by women of fashion. When we have so much that is beautiful in the world of jewellery to choose from it seems cnrious that we should seek the repulsive and loathsome and further pay large sums for it. A Housekeeping Hint.—Marble waslestsnds however well kept are invariably found to need a little extea cleaning when the annual" earthquake" season cones round, but the wa-y housewife will regard with suspicion the various preparations guaranteed to remove in a moment or two, every stain and blemish. and/it mieht frequently be added, the polish too ! A -very harmless and yet effective process is as follows : Cover the marble with a paste made of Fuller's earth and water. and leave it for 21 hour'. The marble should -then be clesned in the Ordinary way when all geease resulni and stains will have disappeared.

CRICKETS AND GOOD LUCK.

CI iikets are supposed to bring luck to any dwelling übere they may chance to te, rays a writer in "Farm and Home," and great is the distress among the old folk if a cricket which has been used to ch'rp forsakes the house or buildinc. Some ray that it is a sign of death, while others sy that to kill a cricket is to invite the Angel of Death to your dwelling. Also it is said that if yeu should crurh a black beetle lain will speedily follow. fn Scotland it is a common superstition that, should crickets forsake a house, a death of one of the members of the family may be looked for ; but, should the cricket return, then a piece of good luck may be looked for.

WARRINGTON WOMEN S CIRCLE. Concert at Bold-street.

With the object of raising £lO to complete the amount promised for a larger roffm in which thtt,Warrin;ton Women's Circle may hold its meetings, a concert arranged by Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes, too't place on Saturday evening in Bold-street schoolroom. Mrs. Rylands presided sill was supported by Mrs. Dodds, Mrs. letheren, Mrs. Dunn, Mrs. Forshaw and Mrs. Moore. _ _ _

_ The concert was unusually interesting, and included several novelties. Chief among these were the adapta- tions for two pianos in the form of duets and quartets. In the former Mrs. Jackson Holmes and Miss Clarice Newell were associated and in the piece "Homage to Handel" (Moschlles) these two played with such unanimity of purpo..,e and good taste as to gain an insistent recall to which response was made with Gurlitt's "Marionette Overture." In the "Presto," taken from Mendelesohn's piano concerto, Miss Clarice Newell had the more responsible part,and her execution was marked by a facility and grace which won admira tion from all listeners. The quarte!s were-not quite so steady, the difficulty of keeping four executants en -rapport accounting for an absence of that one-ness Of intention without which the effect of anythinc, that is attempted must be largely discounted. But the best—and it was quite a vivacious and sparkling number—was the " Poet and Peasant" overture in which Miss Fairclough and Mrs. Willett co-operated with the two pianists already mentioned. Then there wis a piano trio (Beethoven) in which Mrs. Haviour aid Mr. Sever .toatt the violin and 'cello parts with Mrs. Jgckson Holmes at the piano, and also a trio for two violins and piano in which the last mentioned lady, Mrs. Willett and Mrs. Havionr joined, both of which pieces chewed points of interest and intelligent and tasteful interpretation. Mr. Sever's cello solos (Tschaikowski) were somswhat uninteresting though they were nicely played, and Mrs. Haviour's playing of the violin solo, a rlmance by Svendsen, was full of poetry and imagination, her inter- pretation of this fine composition having about it a degree of noulfulness that wes contagious and at once touched the audience to s)mpathy. Compb-ting the record of the insti umental music Mr. Rutter contributed two airs as corn t. solos in good style and was loudly applauded. The two vocalists .3liss Mabel Henshall and Miss Dorothy Truslove tvere singularly alike both in their musical temperament and theirchoice of songs, all of which, with the exception of "Bredon Hill " sung by Miss Henshall, were of alight and piquant chancier , and gave unmistakeable pleasure to' the audience. The ! melancholy pathos attaching to "Bredon Hill" was well brought out by Miss Henshall and tbe song served to shew that, although her forte is undoubtedly in voeal music of the quaint and humourous order,her versatility is snch as to include a distinct success in a widely different direction.

During an interval Mrs: Dorms said she had the greatest pleasure in proposing that their best thanks be given to Mrs. Jackson Holmes for thespleudid pro- gramme she had provided that evening, and to all those who Lad provided the work on the stall and the refresh- ments. Pethaps some there did not know that they atarted the Women's Circle last September, and if they had not done what they wanted to do, stil,,there had been some really goon work done. They had a great many members, and she thought that the Women's Circle had already become a power in Warrington. They hoped that it avJuid become a greab power. She should like to make an appeal to the young ladies of Warrington who had gifts and leisure to go and held them in their work. They were very anxious to have a room at each end of the town so that they could get in touch with a class they had not before been•ahle to touch—the working girls who had no interest in life except pleasure, so-called. Some of the pleasure was of a very doubtful character. They wsnted to put some new interest into their lives. Continuing, she said the session closed that night with the concert—except, of course, the Sunday afternoon servicPs—and they were very grateful indeed to all those who bad gone that evening to help them in any way. Mrs. PATRICK then presented Mrs. Holmes with a table centre, the gift of the Circle. • Mrs.LETHEREN,in seconding the resolution, said that Mrs. C. E. Parker, NI rs Armitage, and Mist Beisio Dutton had sent the7r btbst wi.hes for the usefulness and growth of the circle. No one appreciat,Al more than she did all that Mrs. Jackson Holmes bad done that nlght and she cordially thanked her. She coupled with the resolution the name of Mrs. Ilylands. They must not close the session witheld thanking those who hod lengthened the cards and strengthened the stakes of their circle: it was the cons!ant regular work which demanded more of the Master's spirt and sometimes those who worke I behind the scenes did not rec.,ive their full measure of appreciation. It was the careful attention to d ttils that told in the work. (Applause). Mrs. JACKSON HOLMES replying s tid she tlateked them mist heartily for the good wishes and for the table centre.

The St. Pa'il's annual s)le of work was held on Mon- day in the Acsembly Room, Conservative Club. For years this sale has been a great suocess, and the present ons was not an exception.Mrs.P.J.Lord, a former member of the church, but now of Bournemouth, ecclared the sale open. FREE CHURCH FACTS AND FIGURES.

A book that should find a plan on the reference book-shelf by the side of Who's Who and the Clorg,y List is the Free Chtrch Year Book for 1909. This bandy volume is packed from cover to cover with informs: ion regarding the work, ideas and ambitions of Free Churchmen. It contains full reports of the Swansea meetings in addition to a mass of statistics, 13;91 information, lists of Nonconformist schools and colleges, social and philanthropic societies, addressses of officials of the various Free Church Denominations and many other matters of interest to Nonconformists. It is compiled in a readable and interesting manner and is a book that no Free Churchman can afford to be without. The Free Church Year Book is published by the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churcher, Thomas Law, Memorial Hall, E.C.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Election of Chairman and Vice- Chairman.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, when thera were present :—Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Collingwood, Revs. T. 11. Matthews and M. Ryan,. Messrs. G. Mather, A. J. Jolley, W. 'Gill, J. Billinge, A. Broots, W. Houghton,W. C. Brooks, W. Furness, R. Lee, W. Flanagan, R. Bolton, T. Bitchie,R. Forshaw, M. W. Seton, J. Cook, B. L. Bent, A. Bottomley (clerk), H. W. Peacock (assistant clerk), J. F. Acrbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of the Cottage Homcs).

ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN. Mr. W. HOUGHTON moved the re-election of the Rev. T. R. Matthews as chairman of the Board and spoke highly of the way he had discharged the,duties during the rast twelve months. The Board suffered the loss of his services for, a certain time threugh illness, but they were all delighted to cong.atulate him upon his recovery and hoped his health would be well maintained during the coming year. Mr. BILLINGE seconded and said he hoped the year on which they were entering would be one of success, peace and health for their chairman. The motion was heartily agreed to. The Rev. T. R. MArrnEws in returning thanks, said he had thought of refusing to tbkeollice again, but he was assured that it was the unanimous wish of the members that he should continue for the coming year. As to the future, they must not expect too much. 'Blessed is he that expecteth nothing; he shall not be disappointed." He uould, however, do his best to fulfil the Beard's desires..

On the motion of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Mr. BENT, Mr. George Mather was re-elected vice-ch tirman.

Last year the Chairman and Mr. Jolley wora the representatives, but as the Chairman mils unable to attend the Clerk took his place. Mr. FLANAGAN said Mr. Jolley had been to the meet- ings on many occasions and %as a very capable repre- sentative, but the Clerk wes the mouthpiece of the Guardians and ought to be in touch with the Associa- tion. Ije moved that the Chairman and the Cleik be the representatives. Mr. W. C. BROOKS seconded.

Mr. A. BROOKS moved that the Chairman and Mr. Jolley be the delegates. He said the conference was not a place where they went to learn but to construct and no one could gainsay the fact that they could not send enabler representative than Mr. Jolley for that purpose. - - .

Mr. HOUGHTON seconded and suid it was very desir- able to have continuity of representation. The CLERK said he would not like his name to be put to the vote against that of a guardian. Mr. FLANAGAN : With all due respect to the Clerk, I think he ought to allow his name to go before the Board. I hive no feeling eglinst Sir. Jolley ;heis a gentleman I admire. Mr. JOLLEY said, he had been a menitter of the association from its formation. It existed for the pur- pose of defending the rights and privileges of Guardians end of endeavouring to secure improvements in Poor Law administration. Its functions, therefore, were largely of an executive character and continuity was a very important element. There seemed to be ,ft probability,judging from the spizte2ll of the Premier, of some steps being taken very shortly in the way of legislation affecting the interests of Guardians. Mr. Asquith almost said in a recent speenh that next year something would be done, and if that were so, the work of the Association would be mete important than it had been in any previous year of its history. Eventually the CHAIRMAN withdrew his name and Mr. Jolley and the Clerk were appointed repre- sentatives.

Miss BROADBENT : I think it is very gracious of the Chairman.

The Chairman, Vice-Chain-nen, the Chairman of the Padgate Schools Committee, and the Clerk were appointed represe,,t itives at the North-Western and Central Poor Law Conferences.

RUGBY UNION.

The Stockton Heath Club's Record

STOCKTON HEATH'S KEENNESS.

Stockton Heath wound ill; a most successful season on Saturday by defeating Eccles. This is the second season that they have shown a marked superiority over the junior clubs, and their claim for better fixtures appears to be well founded. They have played 21 games won 22, lost one, and drawn one. The only defeat was against the Old Ct stians. who beat them by a try to nothing, and this happened on the day of the Cheshire trial game, when Stockton Heath supplied three of their Lest men. Strangely enough Smith, the player wqo contributed to their defeat, is now playing with Stoclitcm and is ene of their Lest scorers. They have scored 333 points, against 8u by opponents. The principal scorers were Langley, Davies, Smith, Ferguson, Warren, and Isherwood. The second team has been even more successful, having won all their eighteen games and scored no fewer than 418 points against their opponents'33. It. has heen whispered that Stockton Heath are not as gentle in their methods as they might be (says the "Manchester Guardian ") and some of their opponents have gone as far as to refuse to renew fixtures on the ground of roughness. Stockton Math, it should not be forgotten, are trying gamely to uphold the amateur code in a hotbed of professionalism. As a new club ! they cannot be expected'to have traditions to uphold, nor have they neighbours to look up to. It seems rather Pharisaical to throw them over so lightly, and we trust the clubs who have cancelled fixtures will think better of it. A correspondent, who has seen !Stockton Heath pay "severalt times, assures us that ! their play is only keen and not wilfully rough. The three-quarters, he adds, handle the ball much better ! than the majority of local clubs.

===== 0002601_19090424_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
[By Redcap.] ACCOMMODATION MID HOUSING.

(Continued).

While writing on the above question to the Back- yard poultry farmer in last week's article, and speak- ing of complete covering in, I did not intend to con- vey to my readers the necessity of covering "both the top and sides of the run. This is not necessary and it all depends upon the aspect of your yard. If a Southern aspect, then beside the sleeping quarters only a roof is necessary except just- boarding round the bottom a foot high to keep in the litter.. But On the other hand if your run is fairly open to the North or North-East then board up the run accordingly, but in any ease always allow as much fresh air and light as would be consistent with the warmth required for .the comfort of your fowls. If .1 am charged with repetition I ask to be pardoned, but I do reel it necessary to again repeat the fact, that cold winds and wet runs play havoc with wultry ; and success can never be yours if such conditions are allowed.

An eahhen floor for your run is best well beaten down and made firm, then covered with short litter, reserving a corner for fine ashes to be used as a dust bath. And please take note that you must select the sunniest corner of your run for this purpose, as you always find that when the rays of the sun are stream- ing down the birds will take the opportunity for having their bath, because the soil or ashes are then mum and dry and thus easily trickle through their feathers and cleanse them of vermin. Add a good handful or two of powdered sulphur to the line ashes used in the dust bath,f,ir it is a good insecticide. The rest of the information required by the back- yarder will come under the general hints on "Housing and Accommodation."

===== 0002601_19090424_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
GENERAL HINTS

I hope that I mav have influenced the farmer against running huge flocks of mongrels abcTut the farm yard and that in the future a proper system of breeding for good stock will be adopted on the lines, that I have suggested,. and Fiedler permanent or moveable runs be chosen, see to it that the most sheltered position available is used. , Referring back to the running of a large, number on the yard I would not advocate a complete clearance. in this direction, because I know how useful a few fowls are in picking up waste grain etc. , Beside all this one would not wish that the homestead should lose its charms, for a more desolate thing could not exist than a farniyartl where the "cackle of the hen " and the " crowing of the cock" are not heard. But do not overstock, and use a.good strain of cockerel to run with your birds, for it not only improves your stock but adds to their appearance in general. What looks better than a " Lordly Langshan," or a "Sprightly Leghorn," not to speak of other good breeds strutting about and adding dignity to the rest of the flock? I must not pass over this opportunity of informing you of the great need for perfect cleanliness in the poultry house. It is neCessary to lime-wash it at least twice during the summer months. This should be done in the months of May and September, always choosing a dry and windy day for operations, and commencing in the early morning so that it will 'he perfectly dry before your fowls go to roost. A good system for lime-Washing is as follows : -Get a piece of quick lime sufficiently large to mix enough for your requirements, pour on the water and while you are mixing it add about a pint of paraffin. This is also a valu,ible insecticide and the "red mite "will not live where it exists. Then apply the mixtuie while .it is hot, bec .use it ,dries much quicker, and mere readily kills vermin. Take out the perches and clean well all crevices with the lime-wash brush. In case of covered talus in back-yards, and 'where no objection is offered by the landlord, you , will find it beneficial in the end to lime-wash the whole of the sides and ceiling of your run, as it both gives more lightand sweetens the premises., . I have known people Who after giving their poultry houses an annual lithe-wash, thought it not necessary to remove the droppings more than twice a year. This is an erroneous idea 'and no wonder they found that poultry farming did not pay. Just aslinie- -washing is necessary to the sides and roof of a house so also is it necessary that constant- clearing away of the excrement be seen to. Where it is possible this should be done every, morning. It is only a question of a few minutes par house if done_ daily, otherwise the dirt gets trampled about by the fowls and taints the runs and also invites disease.-

Pecause you have had. to remove the nest boxes out of your way you must not-think that they need not ha limewashed ; every part and section of the internal fittings of a poultry house should be thoroughly cleaned by this process at least twice a year. And now a word about the exterior of the poultry house. Recent years have brought about new methods of preserving the outside of poultry houses. lam still an advocate of the old-fashioned method of felt and tar, because a house' so treated is made not only water- tight, but by this system it ensures warmth, and whether you erect your own poultry house or purchase a new one, if it be not covered with felt I recommend that it should be so covered and tarred twice annually. And if you do this it is surprising how much longer they last. It almost requires the patience of Job for one to tar wire netting, but if you care to try it your extra time mid labour in so doing will be well repaid. The best method of doing this is .to drop the roll of netting in a tank of tar before erecting. When This can be done it will last longer by years.

===== 0002601_19090424_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
PERCHES.

It is the wrong method to fix porches on the ladder principle as fowls always like to fret as high up as they can to perch, consequently te ones on the lower perches are contamimkted. Not only so but they are apt to damage their breast bones by alighting on the. hard wooden floor. Therefore, fix your perches on the level about 18 inches apart and not more than 18 inches from the ground. The width- of the perch should be about 2 inches, with the edges rounded off. Irrespective of lime-washing as already stated, it is a good plan to frequently take the •perehes out of the sockets and if the " red-mite " be •found therein, soak with paraffin, for this is the commonest lodging place in which to 'find these detestable pests. In conclusion any points that I may have omitted can be dealt with in the query column if My 'readers will only make good use of it. The chief 'points on Housing and Accommodation hive, however been touched upon, and I hope that this useful information will be adopted with profit to readers of the Poultry Column. Next week I shall write an article on 'Foods and Feeding:.'

QUERIES :

E.A.B. asks for a cure for " scaly leg"? AxsivEn.—As this complaint is caused by a " parasite " underneath • the scales of the leg, it is necessary to first moisten the scales by bathing in hot water, then apply a mixture of powdered sulpher and unsalted lard. This is best got into the joints of the sales with a tooth brush. Then on the second day apply a dressing of paraffin oil in the same manner. Frequent applications are necessary until an improve- ment is noticeable. After• that apply a dressing of Vaseline to take away soreness that may have been caused by using the brush. INQUIRER complains of 'shell-less eggs ; and would like tq, know what to give the fowls to assist them in shelling their eggs ? AsswEit.—Give your fowls plenty of shell grit and old mortar, but rigidly abstain from giving them new mortar or quick-lime as both are very injurious.

RT3BY FOCTBALL.

WARRINGTON FINISH WITH A DEFEAT.

The Warr:ngton men played their last match of the season en Saturday when they took a weak team to Rochdale. Fish, Shows and Ta3lor were among the absentees, Int the Hornets were at full strength. Rain fell heatily throughout the match, and the ground was in a ferirful state, good footl all beisig practically impossible. About 2,000 spectators attended. The teams were as follow : _ _

Rnchd de Horoe,s —Farrar, b tck : Hart ley, Threl fall, Robinson and West, three-qnarter backs; Boxter and Schofield, half backs; Dearden; Richardson, Fitz- simmons, Ashton, Ashworth, and Pearson, forwards. The replayed tie between Oldham and -Halifax was piayed at Oldham on Wednesday evening and resulted in a win for Oldham by 8 points to 2. Halifax were without Hilton, and though the g line was largely of a forward character they were seldom dangerous. Oldham sco.red nrst through G. W. Smith marking, and Wood placing a goal. THE MQRALIZATION OF

ECONOMIC RELATIONS. PAPER BY MR. C. E. PARKER.

The following is the text of the paper read I,y Mr. O. E. Parker last week before the Social Service Congress at Oxford, and to which reference was made by Mr. Parker in his letter to the " Examiner" last Saturday.

We all admit that the moral aspect of every question is far more important than the industrial and economic. It is the ethical or moral which approves or condemns with a finality from which there is no appeal, any course of human action. It has been finely said that "It is the Christian and holy ethic which has the reversion bf the long economic future." To adapt Emerson's figure we must hitch this waggon to the stars. The history of the development of the race shows very .clearly that moral forces have ever been the most powerful. And yet in their own sphere, economic .considerations must naturally fill a large place. The main object of industry is to produce. We cannot expect to be able to lift this department of life out of the region to which it belongs. It is good that we should lutc4 the waggon to the stars, but after all the waggon must remain on the ground and fulfil the purposes of a- waggon. But we have a right to demand that in

THE CONDITIONS OF LABOUR there should be nothing that will debase man's higher nature, that nothing shall lie introduced that will limit the free development of the best within man. This is the test that we should apply to present industrial conditions.

It has been the belief of thinkers in every age that the particular time in which they live is the most critical period in the world's history. Like those of the rest m y we not indulge the fancy that the present time is the most momentous that ever the world has known. Industrial develop- ment, aided by applied science, has during the past century transformed the conditions of work. Old landmarks have been, and are being, submerged. . Social and industrial forces, new, strange, and altogether immeasurable, have been released among us. 'One. hundred years ago nations and com- munities were divided by almost inseparable barriers; since then the ends of the world have .been brought together, and civilised society is becoming one vast highly organized and inter-dependent whole. The old bonds of society, of nationality, have been loosened, old forces are becoming extinct, new ones are coming into play: Vast displacements of population have taken place and are still taking place: the inhabit- ants of all civilised countries are becoming massed together in towns; there has been a great indraught from the country. The result of the development of the industrial system is, that people have become divorced from the land from which all Sustenance must come; in contact with the land there was always a certain security for labour. To the able and efficient the industrial development has brought. sub- stantial benefit. Work has been lightened, hours of labour shortened, remuneration increased, and the . purchasing power of actual wages largely augmented.. But these gains have been to some extent balanced by certain losses. The workers have in many occupations been de-individualized by being forced to be occupied with some Small detail in the huge mill of industry, and We are asked, what avail is it that the waste places of the earth have been turned into highways of comenerce if many still want the necessities of life? What does it profit if knowledge grows from more to more, and the race as a whole establishes more firmly its control over nature's forces, if all the appliances of science, _ and all the result of industry does not enable those at the bottom of the social ladder .to rise proportionately, and if it con- demns many of them to a state of helpless poverty as pitiable as any which has existed in the past. The great industrial army has moved forward, all the ranks are in good marching order, but outside this disciplined and able-bodied force there are a large number who have become camp followers, who have dropped behind because they cannot keep pace.; many of these, doomed from their birth to be inefficient, who hobble along with tired steps and wearied- hearts, obliged to subsist on what they are able to pick up from the waste thrown aside by the great army, once they have fallen behind seldom being able again to take their place in the ranks.. Their numbers frequently augmented from additions from the regi- ments in the great army, not alone from the rank and file but from officers as well, who from evil of heart,Or weakness of mind or body, cannot keep their place. The conditions of life at the lower end of the social scale are a standing reproach and challenge to the general progress. To what extent is society respon- sible for such conditions, and what must be - done to , alter them? THE QUESTION our, CIVILISATION •11.4 S TO FACE is how these inefficients, the members of this half- starved undisciplined band, can be, reinstated in the ranks and made partakers of all the benefits which arise from the forward march. As Dr. Lidgett would put it : "How can these be made heirs of the civilisation of which they are now the victims 2" The question is a very serious one, the most pressing question the age has to face; the answers to the question are at present necessarily vague :and' confused. It /is not my intention to deal in detail with the minor .questions which are suggested by the indus- trial developments connected with the names of Cadbury, Lever, Rowntree, &c., or to inquire into the effects of co-operation and other excellent movements. There are more important questions .which hold the' thought of the world at tile present time. Our subject further suggests a wider scope than simply the question of the poor and inefficient at the bottom of the social ladder, although- we will agree that the con- dition of these is one which claims the attention of every lover of his country and his kind. It will be admitted that the thought of the present days is being focussed not alone on questions of alleviation, but -rather on the larger question of the basis of economic conditions created and controlled by what we know as the present competitive system. It is not so much a question of pity, or of charity, but 'the larger and more important question.of economic justice, of how far present industrial conditions secure for the individual Members their moral rights. As an illus- tration of what I Mean I would present perhaps the most important of these larger questions as set forth by Herbert Spencer. lam told that this philospher in later life somewhat modified his vieWs, but .am disposed to think that his first conclusion were the more correct. He introduces the subject as follows : • "Ethical truth is as exact and is peremptory as physic ii truth ; and that in this- matter of land tonure, the verdict of morality must be distinctly 3-ea or nay. Either men }nye a right to make the Toil private property, or they have not. There is no medium. We must choose one of the two . positions. There can be no half-and-half opinion. In the nature of things the fact must be either one way or the other.!' Spencer goes on •to contend that private monopoly- of land involves moral strong, and concludes that "it may by-and-by be perceived, that Equity utters dictates to which we: have not yet listened ; and men may then lelrn, th It to deprive others of their rights to the use of the earth, is to commit a crime igferior only in wickedness to the crime of takinetlwa.y their lives or personal liberties." It is not my province to go into the details of the land question, but its moral bearing as here suggested by this authority indicates its close relation to the subject of our paper. Spencer's contention of the moral wrong possible through private ownership, arises out of thelict that land must necessarily be a monopoly and that

THE- RIGHT OF PRIVATE OWNERSHIP IN I.AND

' is subject to a higher, or countervailing. right of access to the means of production because by access alone can human life be maintained. Socialists carry the matter a stage further and declare that not only is land a monopoly, but capital also, and that no real moral improvement can be brought about in economic conditions until both land and capital are nationalised because, they contend, both produce unjust conditions. The questions here raised are momentous ; they go to the very roots of the morality of present day economic relations. Before proceeding • further I Would desire to urge upon • my hearers the necessity of an adequate preparation before any solution of these questions. is . even attempted. What impresses me is, that very many: of those who confidently put forward their schemes do not • seek to attain preparatory knowledge. of the problem they seek to. solve, or that mental equipment whici is all essential. We want insight,and far sight, and wide sight. I would specially emphasise the necessity of - a clear definition of terms: Many Reformers land themselves in a morass because they have no exact idea of what they mean by the terms they enip!oy. .As an example I would instance the confused way in which the terms capital, land, and labour, are employed.. I have by the help of labour. and capital, and by the use of land built in the country a number of five roomed houses which I can let at ss. a week. If any of those rooms were in some parts of London they would let at ss. The rooms which I let for ss, would bring in a weekly return of

255. in London. Twenty shillings.of this goes as rent of land and ss. interest on capital, that is. the labour cost in building. It may answer the purpose in ordinary business affairs to speak of the two owners of these rooms as getting a return on capital, although we add to the confusion by calling a return from buildings rent. But in economics we must recognize the real. difference of rents in the two cases arises almost entirely from the land monopoly, and in examining these questions it is essential that we differentiate between the two. We frequently hear the term

CA.PITILIST MONOPOLY" but it is questionable whether capital can ever become monopoly. It is true it frequently appears to excercise the functions of monopoly, but this is only in appearance, and occurs when capital is exchanged for, or entrenches itself behind, the land monopoly. People speak of the capitalist monopoly as the cause of certain evils apparent in our present system; it is doubtful if capital is in any way responsible, but that the evils we recognize are really parasitic growths which we have allowed to develop around it, and the confusion arises from a want of clear thought and expression. The monopoly of capital is surely a misnomer, or at any rate it has only come into existence through association with natural monopoly such as land. How can there be monopoly in capital if there be equality of access to nature ? When I speak of equality of access Ido not mean that everyone should have his piece of land to go to, but that the State should maintain equal rights, and that an individual who desires more than his share should pay for it so that the benefits should he equally shared. Now since capital is labour stored in material things, were the right to material things made equal, stored labour could not become a monopoly. Were we able to break the grip of monopoly we should be able to restore labour to its rightful position as master in its own house, as employer of capital and not as at present when capital employs- labour. Theoretically the case for land nationalization I believe to be unanswerable-, although in practice it may be found necessary to attain the end by taxation with the object of absorbing the unearned • increase. It may be reasoned that capital can in practice become IV monopoly even if there were established equal rights of access to nature. Instances might be cited of great aggregations of capital bnng used to crush out smaller manufacturers simply by the power of the long purse, and when this is done, capital whether it was a monopoly or no, could assume the functions and power of a monopoly. This I admit, but contend that this does not necessarily, closely touch the question of the workers whose con- dition calls for our consideration and- hns suggested this discussion. The injustice inflicted is upon com- peting individuals in a much higher rang of the -industrial ladder; Is it not quite possible to deal with an abuse of the power of capital by legislature, or perhaps what is more potent the power of an enlightened 'public. opinion? But what I would like to point out is that the most glaring instances of this unjust use of capital almost invariably develop in these cases where capital allies itself with and gets an unjust power from land monoply, for example such com- panies as the Standard Oil Company. The power of such Corporations is derived very largely from the monopolization of some natural product. As a pre- liminary to any really scientific investigation of the subject .we must determine the effect of labour, capital and land separate/ y and estimate the influence of each in relation to the competitive 'system. In con- sidering the evils allied with the competitive system we must carefully differentiate the effects of the separate factors. • It is possible in viewing this question to be so close to some of its aspects as to be incapable of, see- ing the whole in proper perspective. On the one hand there are those_ who spend a self sacrificing life among our poor and unfortunate brethren, who become so impressed with the apparent injustice, with the misery and wretchedness which exist under present conditions that it is little surprise if such attribute it to competition and condemn the whole system in no measured terms and with no discrimina- tion. On the other hand many who are in the midst of

' that you ought to help a weaker brother, and as you all do not do so, we will not give you the opportunity, we the State will take possession of what surplus you produce, and we, not you, will distribute it to those in need. It arbitrarily takes from an individual what is his own, and gives it to his neighbour, and so far con- flicts with one of the most elementary of moral laws. If you want to take all the soul out of a deed Of love, hand it over to paid_ officialism controlled from a central national organization. There is in this a serious limitation .of the . possibility of the freewill offering from individual brotherly hearts,- and in so far as anyone is obliged to do under the stress of compulsion that which he would not otherwise freely do, he is not morally active. You cannot moralize economic con- ditions by seriously narrowing the field in which individual moral activity can find expression and development. Governmental distribution is a poor mechanical Substitute for the free play and love of sacrifice springing from the freewill of the individual. Under Collectivism a great deal of the strength of the old imperatives would become hopelessly enfeebled, and nothing enduring put in _their place. Socialism would demand our attention with more authority if Socialists would first convince us that the present system is not capable of improvement. I dissent from the assumption that existing conditions in which com- petition is an important factor, must necessarily pro- duce the evils we all deplore. They are not its natural growth, but are parasitic and therefore capable of removal. 1 believe it to be possible to retain the advantages which the element of competition gives in industrial affairs, and by wise legislation, to restrict and contiol it so as to make it incapable of producing the evils of which we are all conscious.

The State has for long guaranteed to the individual the enjoyment of his possessions, maintained individual rights, and individual freedom. , The laws of the country are very largely the expression of the moralisation of economic relations. Even freedom itself has been curtailed when that freedom has been recognized as imperilling the welfare of the 'State and the well-being of the ipdividual. . .

Beginning—with the Factory Acts and coming up to the Bill now before, Parliament to prevent sweating, we have a long series of laws which have tended to the moralisation of economic couditions by the limitation of competition. I contend that the com- petition can be so controlled as to become subservient to the good of all, instead of as at present producing a mixed result of good and evil. In a word, to make it a useful servant instead of a cruel master as it is to many.

We do not require to go far back in the history of our country to arrive at a time when Competition and sports were allied together in cock-fighting, bull baiting and when in pugilism men fought with bare fists, or • we might go back still further to the gladiatorial sports when the only alternative to victory was death. But because in these competitions cruelty and brutality were in evidence, no-one could contend that competition in sports was an evil thing and should be eliminated. What has been eliminated is the cruelty and brutality which was associated with thcse sports. I cannot at all see why, in a -somewhat similar- manner, by legal enactment, and by advance in public opinion and in morals, the injustice and the cruelty we find associated with competition in indus- trial affairs cannot be eliminated, indeed we have been eliminating it. by legislation ever since the introduction of the Factory Acts.

WE REQUIRE ABOVE ALL THINGS more general distribution of wealth ; the limitation of all monopolies and privileges which enable some to grow enormously wealthy at the expense of others; we need to advance farther in the direction of protec- tion of the weak and incapable. We require a further limitation of the amounts which can be handed down by inheritance, and in the accumulation of great Wealth in the hands of individuals by a further extension of the principle of graduation in the income tax: But on the other hand we should safeguard the individual so that he may possess and enjoy the fruits of his labour and leave an adequate motive for his toil and saving. Human progress depends on many sided expansion, allowing to the individual free and unimpeded activity so that his varied capactities, aims, and, interests, can be realized and developed in the important realm of toil. And nothing aids so much in this realization and development as healthy competition; Beware of paralizing the springs of individual action by taking away from the individual the right to be consulted as to the terms upon which he is to be expected to give of his best.- With all the faults of the present system, broadly a man works for himself, his wife and his little ones whom he know.s and who are dear to him ; to fancy that be will work for an abstract thing called the State which has little direct, personal interest to him, is to hope for the unnatural and impossible, even if we could guarantee absolute justice in those who were able to grasp the reins of authority and power. Some one has well said that the -nationalization of all would be the de-characterization of all, because it would deny, the distinctive value of component _ part.s which only unite through an agreement to differ."

INDIVIDCALISM ANT) COLLECTIVISM ought to be complimentary and not conflicting principles. The strongest Individualist will be bound to admit that there should be limits to individual action. In the same way Collectivists will admit that Collectivism has its limitations, and that the individual must be left free in certain spheres of life, especially in the realm of the home and family. With this admission on both sides,- the only question is how much of the affairs of life should be governed collectively and how much individually ? and I freely admit that there is ample room for legitimate differences of opinion in regard to this. Probably a great many in England who call themselves Socialists would, shrink - from the extension of the principles as advocated by many who consider themselves as intellectual Socialists, and who look upon the application of the principle to industrial affairs as on insignficant and unimportant step towards the realization of what they conceive to be true Socialism. For my own part I cannot conceive that an, absorption by the State ,of all the means of production, and a management by the State through. a central authority would conduce to The Moralization of Eco.nomical Relations or to their efficiency. . •

===== 0002601_19090424_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMPETITIVE SYSTEM

but are not brought into frequent contact with those at the bottom of the social scale, when they hear strong and unmeasured condemnation of the com- petitive system from a section of the platform, press, and pulpit, can scarce recognise it as the one in which they are taking an active and healthy part, and fancy it must belong to some other world. Were such to 1 describe the cempetitive system they might liken it to a game say of cricket in which while both sides con- test-for the mastery, all the players derive health and pleasure. They thoroughly enjoy the competition, and were it removed they would miss much of the vim and interest of the game. They would claim that com- petition develops in the highest degree worthy qualities which are brought into play endurance, skill, forethought and enterprise, which would not be so developed were it removed. If we could imagine a game in which the competitive element were eliminated we should have a condition similar to that which would exist in business if this so-called " cut thro " competition were lacking. Suppose in cricket the runs were never scored, and in football the points never recorded, how much of the interest and zest of the game would be gone.- In games as in business there are sometimes those who meet with misfortune and are put out of play. If this occurs the game is stopped and the member attended to, so to a large extent in business if a man is badly hurt by the competition his competitors are the first to help-- . that is if the man has played. the game. May I suggdst, if the competitive system could be so con- trolled as to place it in a position somewhat akin to that we have in competitive games, that this would, go far to remove the strong objection of those who would abolish competition altogether and replace the system ' by a vast association of which the -State was the central administrative body. I very. much doubt if there is any real well-thought-out objection to com- petition in industrial as in other affairs. In the minds of many, the objections. which: are raised against competition arise out of the knowledge of the fact that. certain persons by the acquisition of wealth, in. various forms gain this at the expense of their • fellows, and therefore a system which admits of this possibility must be r:dically unjust and should be swept away. Thus we have the idea of the com- petitive system being made unjust by its association with what is loosely called the capitalist monopoly. Now here we come to the difficulty suggested by the want of clearly defined terms requiring general cousent and understanding. I greatly covet the faith of those who arc able to believe that somehow or other there would be developed under a system of nationllised industries some force which would be as efficient as that of individual necessity, which although at present labour- big under conditions which are to some extent unfair, is after all the motive power which urges everyone to do his best. I envy the trust which some are able to place in the effectiveness of a centralized governmental industrial system. .I would gladly recognise that some of THE IDEALS ADVOCATED DV SOCIALISTS •

are in many of their expressions high and noble, and that these_ ideals are akin to, and inspired by, the teachings of the Christian religion. All that is good in Socialism is derived from Christianity, and for such ideals I do not .see much advantage in changing the name Christianity into Socialism. But while we can appreciate the ethical undertone of which much of the teaching of Socialism is without doubt the expression, we are not compelled to adopt the definite proposals of Socialism in respect to the •nationalising of all the means of production, unless such a proposal appeals to us as economically sound. To my mind these pro- posals are unwise, risky, and would, I believe, prove more wasteful than the 'present system in which glaring instances of waste can no doubt be brought forward. Competition may be . wastef Ivti 11 in material it things, but collectivism woule be teful in a far more vital point viz. in deadening the springs of - individual activity. The generating power of which 'human beings are capable (if then- powers are free and unimpeded) will always produce more than the same powers confined and controlled by an absolutism,and it matters not if this supreme authority has its origin in democracy. The influence of the disturbance of natural motive to labour is a factor which should be very carefully considered. But I Must leave the economic and confine myself to the aspects more closely allied to the moral, although I find the two almost inseparable. I cannot see how under the Socialistic system you can guarantee to the worker the full enjoyment of all he produces aud doubt if any form of Socialism aims at this. And yet I contend that it is the only moral basis upon which a stable civilization can exist. It is tic) answer to retort that present conditions do not give to every worker the enjoyment of what he . produces. What its opponents have to prove to us is that it is incapable pf providing the conditions mit of which the just recom- pense will 'naturally be given. In every Socialistic proposal that I havo seen brought forward Socialism 'does not suggest, but it compels men to help each other through the State, nay, it goes further and says

RAILWAY EX,SUPERINTENDENrS DEATH.

The death took place on Saturday at Lockerby-road, Fairfield, Liverpool, of Mr. Jornes Shaw, formerly Northern superintendent of the London and North- Western Railway. Mr. Shaw was nosily 84 years of age. His first tailway connection was with the East Lanca- shire line, which later was merge 4 into the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway. In 1867 Mr. Shaw joined the London and NorthAVestern Company as district superintendent, and after serving in that capacity 32 years retired on a pension. He frequently had charge of Royal trains, and on his retirement Queen Victoria presented him with an inscribed silver cup.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, APRIL 24TH, 1009.

Mr. F. E. SMITH 15

DISESTABLISH- one of the party MENT IN WALES. humouiists. Although his manner serious enough on Wednesday there virs-5 something decidedly like humorous exaggeration in his description of the Bill introduced by the Prime Minister as " measure which attacks one of the sources of national idealism, and deliberately tends to the weakening of morality." Such language' as this has no sort of applicability to the case. It is an insult to the people of Walo to suggest that their morality is ill any way dependent on a Church to whi only a small proportion of them belong, and it is an insult to that Church itself to repre- sent that its services, such as they are, to tho Welsh people, would cease as soon se Disestablishment took place. It may be readily admitted, as it was very freely and fully admitted by Mr. ASQUITH, that tho Church of England in Wales is a much more admirable body at the present time than it has been at any previous period in its history. It is no doubt true tha within recent years it has opened fl new life, and has endeavoured faith- fully to perform its duties. But it was too late, as the PRIME MINISTER remarked, ill

learning the lessons of the past. It had per- manently alienated- a people with a peculiar'

religious temperament. There is now, sal Mr. As@Trim, an enlightened ecclesiastic

statesmanship and a strong spirit of devotioll among the clergy; but the ground that might have ISeen secured in the past canllo

be won. In these days the English Churell in Wales is one of the weak spots of the Principality, and inevitably it is through it°

- Fie position of privilege an offence to

national spirit. Mr: ASQUITH presented the case for hi° Bill as .the strongest that has ever beell placed before any legislators,and the descrir tion is one that seems to us to lie fillll

deserved. Whilst the Church in Wales 11-5,? been growing more fully alive to the rea' nature of its position, and has inclined to much more serious view of its responsi" bilities, it has actually weakened in influelle° over the Welsh. Out Of thirty-f 011,1.0 members for Wales there is hot one viu is not in favour of Disestablishment. lii great industrial town like Cardiff it been able to attach to itself no more tl°; one out of twelve of the population. throughout Wales 193,000 communicants, against more than half-a-million commurii- c.ants attached to the four principal. Welsh sects. , Whatever test be ,applied—that of contributions, of Sunday schools, or of the amount, of accommodation provided .in the churches—the result is always- the same. he English Church in Wales stands out as an even more gross anomaly than was the Established Church in Ireland. Welsh Non- conformity, as the Prime Minister pointed is something quite different in its ongin and its character to the Non- 'conformity of England. It did not arise out of a Puritan revolt. Welsh Noncon- formity was the product of a revolt based not on dogmatic differences or (lisPutes as to ceremonial things, but on a deep distaste and general condemnation of the uses to which the Church was put. All The offices in the Establishment were held by Versons who were out of touch with Welsh 'feeling, and who in many instances saw little 'of the Wels-h people. The case of RICHARD 'WATSON, Bishop of Llandaff, is a typical one. ir his Bishop, an able man, the opponent of 711°34As PAINE, lived on a rarge estate in "k-almberland, and considered it no part of his duty to visit his diocese. It became neces- 'sar Y in the interests of religion to separate -trern the Church, as the Welsh people did, iVing up much more in 'doing so than the Present race of Churchmen is asked to sacri- tce through Mr. ASQUITH'S Bill. Mr. F. E. SMITH says that the financial 13roPosa1s of the measure mean spoliation. iardly any word could be less accurately •Zescriptive. The interests of present incum- -"lris are to be carefully guarded. There Will be a Church representative body to deal lvith, endowments when Disestablishment bas taken place, and there will be the most 'careful safeguarding of public and private lights. Of course the four Welsh Bishops cease to sit in the House of Lords, but ,at will surely 'conduce to an increase rather an a reduction of their pastoral efficiency. Ile, PRIME MINISTER gave it as his honest and deliberate opinion that the Church will I,filld that she will gain more than she loses Disestablishment, and that her appeal under the new conditions, be made more „irectlY to the hearts and the sympathies of 'lle People of Wales. That, we believe, is a 't,onviction that would be generally held if _ripe subject could be looked at without heat tem- per and apart from the bias of self- interest.

The Examiner, Saturday April 24, 1909.

The Opposition try to persuade themselves that the Liberal Party have re-assembled at Westminster in gloom and depression, dis- heartened 'and despairing of the future. That certainly was not the spirit 'which breathed through the Prime Minister's great speech at Glasgow. If "all the terrible risks and catastrophes" which the Govern- ment's opponents "see looming upon the horizon " were realities, still the Govern- ment would not falter in its determination to grapple with the great social problems which, together with national defence, constitute the chief task of statesman- ship here as throughout the civilised world at the present time. But so far from showing any symptoms of dis- couragement and lassitude, the mood of the Government, as evidenced by Mr. Asquith's survey of the work before it, is conspicuous for'buoyancy and optimism. In the three years of its existence it has already effected far greater achievements in social reform than the record of the Conservative Party can show during a quarter of a cen- tury. Undaunted by the sharp struggle which is imminent in a Session that promises to be men'iorable for finance as bold and efficient as the measure which placed Sir William Harcourt in the front rank of Chancellors of the Exchequer, the Liberal Party is already looking forward to 1910. There is every reason to believe that in the next Session of Parliament the iLiberal Government will, as Mr. Asquith suggests, write still another chapter, the most fruitful which has yet been devised or contem- plated in the history of !the improvement of the people's conditions of life.

THE GOVERN- MENT'S OPTIMISM.

The nioney troubles of PAYING FOR the German Govern- THEM. ment have, of course, been a subject of com- ment here for months. The Liberals wanted death-duties, the Conservatives (who, with them, form Prince Bulow's bloc) would not have them, while the Liberals refused to countenance any further taxes on the poor. The Government, in desperation, suggested absurd taxes on electric light and power and advertisements. Needless to say, the Finance Committee rejected these as economically ruinous, and quite incapable of making up the huge £25,000,000 deficit. At last it is announced that an agreement has been patched up; both sides have conceded something, the Liberals, apparently, more than the others. Taxation on articles of general consumption is to raise £17,000,000 or £18,000,000, fresh taxation of property (including an extension of the legacy duties) £5,000,000. The rest of the money required is to be obtained by an additional impost on railway. tickets. The Germans are a patient race, and can stand a good deal. But one cannot help, thinking that every time the ordinary German citizen eats a meal or enters a booking-office to buy a ticket he will think of Dreadnoughts. Practically all these additional and extremely obnoxious taxes are necessitated solely by the enormous naval expenditure decidedupon last year. Germans have been accused of a good many things, but nobody has ever accused them of not being able to put two and two together. A little more of this, and they will be approaching us to ask , for a diminution of armaments.

There is good prospect

THE of.the Town-Planning TOWN-PLANNING Bill passing this session.. INVESTIGATION. Special interest, there- fore, attaches to the, investigation of town-planning in Germany, conducted during the holidays by a party of eighty representatives of municipalities and other English bodies concerned in the administration of the Act. The principal fact which emerges from the inquiry is the necessity of framing every scheme with due regard to local conditions. It is manifestly impossible to- apply to a North Country manufacturing town the double row of trees down the centre of the road which is to be found in some Continental cities. If a footpath, a cycle path, and a motor-track occur between the trees in some up-to-date German towns, it is clear that these luxuries are not for immediate and wide imitation in this countiw. But there is much that English town-planners may learn from the German towns. The circumstance has been made clear by the inquiry that it is not, in Germany at any rate, the landlords who oppose town-planning schemes. This has encouraged the English municipal reformers. Landlords realise that town- planning is the best thing that can happen to then'. It sends up the value of the local property, and its tendency is both to raise the value of land and to raise rents. Indeed, therein is one of the occasions of criticism. The provision of cottages becomes a more difficult affair, and huge tenements of such a kind as one sees in London, and, indeed, all large towns, take their place. This is hardly conducive, especially under the conditions which obtain in Germany, to the cultivation of home life. Here, however, comes in the advantage of that adaptation to local con- ditions which the returned deputation are ready to advocate. This is a highly import- ant and a highly technical work, and no architect can be too good to devote his talents and research to it. The extension of towns and the building of houses must pro- ceed according to the best architectual knowledge. It is time that we had done with haphazard,and that the best experience in the laying out of towns was at the disposal of every municipality, and was being worked out in practice in every town in this country.

NOTES. AND NOTIONS.

• The westerly wind on Tuesday last helped to bring the tidal wave up the river and at the hour of high tide the water had been raised to a height scarcely parallelled in recent years the 'water overflowing freely upon the New Road and converting Arpley and Cuerdley meadows for a few hours into large inland lakes.

* * We regret to hear that owing to the great pressure of political engagements, Mr. Ure, the Lord Advocate, finds it impossible to address a meeting at Warrington in May. He has, however, definitely promised to speak here at a later date when we are sure that local progressives will eagerly avail themselves of the opportunity of hearing this great exponent of the case for land values taxation.

"Would tariff reform be of benefit to the iron and steel workers ?" There is, we hear, every prospect of a public debate on this interesting and important question taking place between Mr. J. Elwell, jun., and Coun- cillor James Broadhurst. The meeting will be held in the Thewlis-street schools at a date to be arranged and admission will be by tickets which will be at the disposal of the two parties. It has-been suggested that the Speaker of the Parliamentary Debating Society should be asked to take the chair. 40 The unearned increment attaching to land values has been strikingly demonstrated recently in the City of London by the acquisition by the Corporation of a site which has for a long period been occupied by a fruit stall in Ship Tavern Passage, Gracechurch- street, at approximately £3O per foot, or £1,050, inclusive of the costs. This indicates the remarkable value of land near the centre of the City. The price paid here is at the rate of £1,306,800 per acre, which shows that our country and City have still substantial assets against national and local liabilities. _ _

Mr. Alfred Drury, the eminent sculptor who carved the best of King Edward VII. which stands in the vestibule of the War- rington Town Hall, has just completed an heroic figure of the Prince Consort. It is a fine piece of work and will be placed in its position over the main entrance to the new South Kensington Museum, in the course of a few days. Carved in Portland stone, the statue is eight feet high,and those who have seen it consider it among the famous sculptor's happiest efforts. It will be supported by ail architectural pedestal. An accompanying panel contains the figures of Queen Vicloria St. Michael and St. George, Knowledge and Inspiration, all of which have grown under Mr. Drury's hands, and are now in the places reserved for them. * *

The insuperable difficulties of settling the religious education controversy on any other but a secular basis are frequently illustrated by incidents from the schools which are more potent than argument. Dr. Clifford tells the following story in the " Baptist Times" :—" A child taught in a Council school is, along with some other children, misbehaving at home, and mother says, What naughty children you are!' and the child answers, 'I know why we are naughty; it's because we have not been chistened.' The startled mother says, Who told you that ?: and the child replies, Teacher says if we are christened and have the mark of the Cross on our foreheads we shall he good for ever and ever.' The father who sends this to me vouches for its accuracy." * * *

The Board of Trade return shows that last year there was a decrease of 74 in the number of persons killed on the railways of the country, and 827 in the number injured, compared with the previous year. Satisfac- tory as the diminution is, however, the casualty roll is still a serious one. The twelve months' fatalities numbered 1,043, and the cases of injuries totalled 7,984. Passengers provide only a small proportion of the fatal cases, less than a tenth, in fact. Three railway servants are killed to one passenger. The companies have done, and are doing, all in their pclwer to minimise the risks of their men, but the familiarity which breeds contempt is evidently responsible for the loss of many lives each year. Suicides and other trespassers account for the bulk o the fatalities, last year's number being 479. _ _

All who heard Mr. Ramsay Macdonald, M.P., when he visited Warrington, were agreed that he is a powerful orator, full of enthusiasm for. the cause of which he is such an able and untiring exponent. As President of the recent momentous Conference of the Independent Labour Party at Edinburgh he was heard to such advantage that Mr. Keir Hardie described him as the "largest intel- lectual asset" the party had. This tribute was evoied by the formal address which Mr. Macdonald delivered from the chair, but more remarkable even than the qualities of this set oration was Mr. Macdonald's manner of guiding and controlling the deliberations of the three hundred not very tractable delegates. It is not in vain that Mr. Macdonald has studied the methods of the of the present Speaker, who may be called the best, as he is the first chairman of the day. These conferences with the widely scattered delegates, and often with a disturb- ing echo in the hall, are difficult to move along their appointed course. A voice which could on occasion bear down all opposition and the clearest of clear heads were the two qualities with which Mr. Macdonald carried it successfully through an exceptionally heavy and intricate programme of business.

A fine old warrior is ,Colour-Sergeant Henry Bond Bowers, 9, Mill-lane, Warring- ton, the subject of this week's "life story." It is given to but few men to have passed through such stirring and epoch-making experiences and Colour-Sergeant Bowers is justly proud of his record and of the well- won medals which adorn his breast. He 'was one of that contingent of intrepid, tireless heroes who gained renown as " Havelock's little band." In the full glare of the scorching sun they tramped, mile aftef mile on their errand of succour to Cawnpore and Luck- now, and although often outnumbered, they carried positions which seemed impregnable and put a determined enemy to flight. The old 64th Regiment, to which Colour-Sergeant Bowers belonged, acquitted itself with such gallantry as to win the special commend- ation of General Havelock. For forty years the Colour-Sergeant has drawn his pension and we hope he may long continue to enjoy the bounty which he has so fully earned by service to his country. To hear him recite "The Gallant 64th Regiment" is to realize something of the horrors of Cawnpore, for he throws his whole soul into it with a fervour inspired by the memory of comrades, who fell on the field in recapturing that hap- less town.

MOTOR CAR, FATALITY. Boy. Killed at Sankey Bridges

Liverpookroad, Sankey Bridges, was the scene of a shocking motor car fatality on Wednesday evening, the victim being William John Coley, aged nine years, son of William Thomas Coley, labourer, 29, Plimpton- street, Sankey Bridges. The car is owned by Mr. j. G. Withinshaw, Penketh, but the only occupant at the time of the accident,Avas the chauffeur,George West, of Chapel-road, Penketh. It appears that the boy was crossing the road near Barnard-street and when about

half-wa—y across he hesitated and suddenly--Woi-aPed—, with the result that the motor-car, which was travelling at a very moderate speed in the direction of Penketh, knocked him down and passed over him. Deceased was taken to the Warrington Infirmary in the dr, and died at 9.30 the same evening from a fractured skull, never having regained consciousness. An inquest' will be held at the Polices Station at 10.10 on Monday morning.

NATIONAL SCHOOLS EXTENDED. Denominational Teaching Upheld. The formal opening of the extension of the National Schools, Church-street,took place yesterday afternoon in the presence of a large number of people. The extension, which cost £l,OOO, had to be made to accom- modate the scholars on a loft, basis according to the rcquirements of the Board of Education and not on an Bft. basis as formerly. The school's average attendance is now 630. During the opening the boys rendered 1 songs. The MAYOR, in declaring the sale open, referred to , the time when he was a scholar at the National School, ' and was thanked for his services at the opening. MR. BROWN (headmaster) proposed a vote of thanks to the managers. Alderman BENNETT seconded, and it was supported by 1 Father WILSON who said that the managers \of the school had earned the gratitude of all those who upheld the denominational principle in education. Perhaps , there had been no time in the latter years of the educa- tional controversy in which it had been so (important ' that those who considered the denominational principle ,in education to be for the welfare not only of the church, bat also for the whole of the nation, should show a firm front as at the present moment. He slid that because of the avowed boasted policy of the present Government. They had dropped actual legislation for the present. It was only a very short time since Mr. Runciman said that those attempts which hitherto had failed, and which were so dangerously threatening the denomina- tional schools, were not altogether done with. ft was quite clear tint the Government so far AR legislation for the present was concerned, were preparing the way for making snob legislation as they desired more easy to accomplish. He bad nothing whatever to say against Council schools. Mr. Bunciroan said that Council schools were what he and his Government would do their best to foster. There were many ways in which the Government could foster the decrease of denomi- national schools. Therefore he felt that all those who, stood for the eenominational principle in educa- tion should feel deeply grateful to the managers of the school for the extension of their buildings. If they would only bear in mind the attitude of the Govern- ment he thought they would be more tenacious of what they considered to be theirs. It behoved them to be tenacious and take their stand strongly. The RECTOR replying to the vote of thanks, said he was satisfied with the working of the 1902 Educition Act. He held that religious instruction should be given in the schools according to the faith of the parents. He was willing to grant to others what he claimed for themselves. A person w,is composed of three parts, the body, mind and soul. They trained the body and mind so they should necessarily attend to the soul. (Applause,)
