MARRIED WOMEN TEACHERS,

School Management Commmittee Firm.

TEACHERS TO CO.

At a meeting of the Education Committee on Monipy next, the recommendations of the School Management Committee on tha question of married women teachers will come up for confirmation. The Committee has passed resolutions to the following effect :

A letter having been read from the Managers of the Fairfield school, stating that they are not prepared to give notice of dismissal to Mrs. Lydia Mary Stansfield, the head mistress of the girls' department of that school, nor to submit any special reasons for the continuance of her services, it was resolved That, in accordance with tke Education Act, 1902, section 7 (1), three months' notice from the Ist day of May, 1909, be given to Mrs. Lydia Mary Stansfield to terminate her engagement as head mistress of the girls' department of the Fairfield school on the 31st July, 1909. Similar resolutions were passed in respect of Mrs. Mary Dooley, Sewing mistress of St. James' School. Mrs. Elizabeth Hankinson, certificated assistant mistress at St. Ann's C.E. School.

A letter having been read from the Managers of the S t. Barnabas' School, stating that they are cot prepared to give notice of dismissal to Mrs. Annie Proud, an nncertificated assistant mistress in the Mixed depart- ment, and to Mrs. Catherine Grounds, a certificated assistant mistress in the Infants' department of that school, as they consider them educationally, morally and physically fit for their work, a similar resolution giving both ladies three months notice was passed. A letter having been read from the managers of the Wycliffe school, stating that notice of dismissal bad not been given by them to terminate the engage- ments of Mrs. Elizabeth Steel Corney, sewing mistress in the mixed department, and to Mrs. Emily Constance Scarishrick, a certificated assistant mistress in the infants' department of that school, the managers being equally divided upon the subject, it was resolved that three months notice, in the same terms as the others be given in each case.

An intimation having been received from the managers of the Sankey Bridgef (Infants') Council school that., having satisfied themselves that there are no special reasons for the continuance of the service of Mrs. Christina Margaret Hayes, uncertificated assistant mistress at that school, they concur in the proposal of the Education Committee to give her three months' notice to terminate her engagement, A resolution giving such notice was passed. A lett3s was read from themanagef the S. Albans R.C. School, in reference to Mrs. Mary Gittoes, a certificated assistant mistress in the mixed department of that school, but consideration of this case was deferred until further information is received. A letter was read from Mrs. Langdon Isabel Ca nn snperintendent of cookery instruction, setting forth, her reasons why her services should be continued, but consideration of her case was deferred until the next meeting of the committee. PROVISION OF MEALS TO BE DISCONTINUED It having been reported to the School Attendance Committee that there were now only 55 children on the registers of the children's dining rooms, the Committee proceeded to consider the question of the continuance or otherwise', of the provision of meals to school children. After some discussion it was resolved, that the provision of meals to school children be dis- continued on and after the 30th April next. Authority was given for the employment of an assistant at thp Centre for preparing the meals for school children during the week, commencing on the 26th April, 1909, at 'a cost of 10s. 6d. It was also resolved that authority be given for the prosecution of another parent with a view to the recovery of the cost of providinr, those of his children who ra in attendance at school' with mid-day meals, such cost baing at the rate of Is. per week per child.

CYCLING.

WARRINGTON CYCLING CLUB.

The opening run of the season to Warburton took place on Saturday last. The weather conditions, as iFt usually the case with the first run, proved to be very disagreelble, it raining the whole of the afternoon. A fair number of a large gathering turned out on the wheel aria th 3 remainder made ,the journey by rail. Warburton reached, the afternoon was spent in numerous attractions which the "White City" there offers. bowling and boating being amongst the number. The event of the afternoon, however, was a skittle c3mpetl- - between sides picked by the Captain and ex-Captain ,which caused much laughter and prolonged amuse- ment and resulted in a win for the captain's side. The winning side ware suitably rewarded for their_ efforts by the' losers. Tea of the excellent nature provided there was taken at 5.45 and ample justize done to it. After tea weather preventing any a. tention to outside lecreation, a concert of an impromptu character lies arranged and greatly appreciated by the members. Several new songs with choruses were given mhich will be added to tip club's repertoire, and probably rendered on occa- sion dur:ng the season. T h return home was maie in good time and War- rington reached sfter a most successful opening run, despite wet weather.

DRINK TRADE STATISTICS.

A. Government Blue-book just published gives the !ollowing facts,

DECREASE IN LICENSES.

The net decrease in the number of licenses in the last four years was :-1905. 584; 1900, 1,340; 1907, 2,037; 1908, 1,382.

COST RISING YEARLY.

Each year since the Act came into operation (says the blue book) shows an increase in the cost of com- pensation for extinguished licenses. In 1995 the cost was for each license £614 and last year it was £994.

INCREASE OF CLUBS.

But though the number of licenses has decreased the number of registered clubs increased from 6,907 in 1907 to 7,133 in 1908-226 increase, or 3 per cent.

DECREASE IN CONVICTIONS.

The number of convictions for drunkenness, and crime combined with drunkenness in 1998 was 187,803, representing a decrease on the figures of 1907 of 9261 or 4.70 per cent., which follows a decrease in previous years. The decreases are spread over , nearly all parts of the country The percentage of convictions of males and females remain about the same and have done so for the last four years.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" The Crimes of Paris.''

"The Climes of Paris " is an al favourite, but hi some respects it scores over many of its modern com- petitors in the dramatic realm. Far-fetched though it may be, the story is one of powerful interest, and there is nothing insipid or boring. Mr. Alfred D. Adams carries off chief honours as Viscount Des Vismes, who' rejoices in the apt sobriquet of the Demon, and inspires terror and Lathing by his unscrupulous villiiny. Mr. Clavering Craig is admirable as Pepin Cardel, the celebrated detective, who is always turning up unexpectedly in a fresh disguis3 and propounding subtle theori,s a la Sherlock Holmes. Miss Vera Clavering Craig gives a toucbing portrayal of Angelo Martel, and all the other parts are capably sus.ained. The mounting of the play is elaborate and evokes great admiration.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Wilson Barrett's famous play " Claudian," pays ri return visit to the Royal Court Theatre next week. It will, as before, be played by Mr. William Maclaren's company. Mr..Maclaren will himself take the role of " Claudian, the Accarsed," and his fine stage presence and powerful but flexible voice enable him to ppurtray the character in all its strength and impressiveness Miss Lily C. Bandmann, daughter of Mrs. Bandmann Palmer, again appears in the role of " Almida." rho principal artistes will he supported by a company fifty- strong, whilst the play will he mounted with faithful attention to detags. the gm...'eons costume being of the period. The staging will be most effective, and the earthquake scene, involving the destruction of Claudian'n Palace, is one of the most realistic scenes ever placed ori the stage. The play has been very popular on its pre- vious visits, and it will doultlesi draw , good houses ; again.

matism ? Dizziness? Pains in the Loins and Back?

Nothing is more to be feared than uric acid poisoning, because it comes on so quietly, and is often unsuspected until it has spread dangerous disease throughout the body. The kidneys' great work is to filter the uric acid out of the blood. But when they are weak and ill they fall behind with their work, and so the blood, burdened with this dangerous poison, carries disease to all parts of the system. This explains how uric acid makes you always tired, impatient and irritable your back is ,bad your head throbs and aches and comes over dizzy the eyes are puffy, the skin yellow you have rheumatic pins in the muscles and.' joints. MR. VICTOR GRAYSON, M.P., AT EARLESTOWN. EQUALITY OF THE CLASSES.

Mr. Victor Grayson, speaking at Earlestown on 111onday night, said he would not occupy much time, as he bad to be at the" Menagerie " to-morrow. He bad borrowed this word from his friend, Sir Charles Dester. As to Dreadnoughts, he said the Liberals said they would only build four, the Tories said they would have eight, and the member for Coln o Valley advised the people to vote at the next general election, which, so be was informed, was to come off after the present session, for the eight, unless the LiberaN offered sixteen. Mr. Grayson went on with the old-age yansions. What a farce this was. Could they have put the age-limit further back then 70 or given the applicants less then ss. per week? One old lady, on receiving her ss. in Liverpool, asked " Who gave me this ?" On being told the Libetal Government she fell down dead —no wonder. lie would undertake to cure anyone of either Liberalism or Toryism in one niklit if they would accompany him in the Parliament Chamber and - see how the business of the country was condncted. Ho described the new play, "The English- man's Home," and the reception it had received in Germany. The men were being literally forced into joining what he termed the" Rotten Territorials." Alderman ligrtley, of Bradford, and Mrs. Bamber, of Liverpool, supported Mr. Grayson in his remarks and attacks on the Government.

NEWTON URBAN bISTRICT COUNCIL,

The annual meeting of this Council was held at the Town Hall, Earlestown on Monday evening when there wore present :—Messrs. T, Ormand, Thompson, C. B. F. Borron, J. Higham, T. Ritchie, M. W. Seton .1. Harwood, W. Davies, E. E. Evans, Sankey, E. L. Bent, L. R. Pilkington, C. Cole, (clerk), Surteea (water engineer), and Bowes (eurveyor). Mr. W. Thompson moved that the best thanks of the Council be tendered to Mr. Ormand for his invaluable services in the chair during the past sear and in previous years. They congratulated Mr. Ormand upon his well deserved promotion to a position of greater responsibility, and trust. (Applause). Although they would lose the benefit of his services in the chair, he hoped that for some time they would still have his valuable help as a member of the Couucil and as chair- man of the Finance Committee. Mr. C. B. F. Borron seconded and several members supported the resolution, which was carried, sni Mr. °mind replied. He afterwards spoke upon the work of the Council dur:ng the past you as to the growth of the district, it was not all that they would like to see. Still, it was as much as they could anticipate tinder the circumstances. Plans for the erection of five new houses and 22 other buildings were passed by the Council during the year ended 31st. March, as compared with 22, the preceding year, and 28 the year before that. The population as estimated by the Registrar-General .to the middle of 1908 was just over 110,000, but he thought he was right in saying that the estimate of the Registrar-General was invariably above rather than below the average, SO that they must take a little discount off that. Just to show the rapid growth of the district, he might say that in 1871, the population was 9,000 and in 1861 it was 6,000. The birth rate. unfortunately, showed a alight diminution compared with last 3ear, although it was slightly above the birth rate for England and Wales generally. Their birth rate was 27.15 against 26.5 for England and Wales. Their death rite was very good, as it was only 11.34,although slightly above the previous year. They would agree

with him the rate was good when pointed out that the death rate for England and Wales was 14.7., On the motion of Mr. Ormand, Fee° decl by Mr. Borron, Mr. William Thompson was elected chairmen of the Council. Mr. Pilkingten was elected to the vice-chairmanship. It was decided that the. whole Council should form the various committees, with the exception of the Store t Committee and the Staff Committee, which will be composed of the chairmen of other committees. M.es4r9. C. B. F. Borron, J. F. Clarke, Ormand and F. J. Wood were re-elected representative governors on the goverbing bodies of the Winwick Grammer School, the Dean School Exhibition Charity, and the Lowton Endowed School. The Ch2irman wag chosen as the r. presentative on the Council of the Society for University Extension in Liverpool and District.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS FARMER'S FAILURE.

William Whittamore, Ravenshead Farm, St. Helens, appeared for public • examination at the Liverpool hankruptcy Court on Monday, with liabilities .£43i and assets £ls9.—Mr. A. E. Tickle appeared for the bank- rupt. uho was examined by the Deputy Official Receiver, and stated that forsome flftten years be had carried on a cab business in St. Helens, which, however, had not been successful, and was closed four years ago, being liquidated tinder the direction of an accountant. There was a considerable balance of liabilities over !Issas, but the bankrupt from the receipts of his farming busi- ness bad contributed the deficiency with the exception of debts to the amount of a few pounds, which were now included in the present statement of affairs. He ascribed his failure in farming to losses arising from the deaths of horses and other causes. He had carried on a milk business, but found this was not remuriera- tive, and ahont a year ago his wife took it over and converted it into a retail business, which she ,a as still carrying on on her own account.—The public examina- tion was closed.

MORE STRIKE -SCENES. MOULDER FINED FOR ASSAULT.

John.McLny, a moulder, of 3. Appleton-street, . was charged at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday wi4h assaulting and beating William Winstanley, a brick- setter, of 62, Dudley-street, and also with haring been drunk on the licensed premises of the Horse and Jockey Inn, Winwick-road, on the 7th Apr 1. The cly‘rge of drunkenness was first proceeded with. Chief Inspector Williams was called, and strted that at one o'clock on Saturday be went to the Horse and Jockey to arrest the prisoner, and found him in the 1 ar sitting among a number of other men. He was undoubtedly drunk, and witness got him away qs quickly as possible. _ _ _ _

In answer to Mr. Jenkins, Who defended, witness said there was no drink in front of prisoner. Prisoner did not look ill or weak, on the other hand he was very strong. On 'arriving at the Police Station prisoner denied that he was drunk. Detective Reddington gave corroborative evidence. and said that whilst on the way to the Police Station prisoner challenged him and another officer to fight. The Chief Constql•le also gave evieence and stated, on seeing the pris mer at the Niles Station, he (pH: oner) challenged witness to fight, and his language. was not altogether complimentary. ' Mr. Jenkins, who defulded, said his instructions were that 'McLay was not suffering from d, unkenness,hut from the effects of want of food. lle hid very little drink, and at noon went to the Horse and Jockey, where the men on strike at Whyman's Foundry had a club room. He took with him a boy to try and raise a subscription to send him to Glasgow' and a collection was made. He (Mr. Jenkins) tbought that was a very sensible action and not one of a drunken man. Then he went into the bar and had a pint of Leer. He was not drunk but exhausted from want of food froni being on picket at Whyman's Foundry for several hours.

William Bell, a moulder residing in Hall-street, gave evidence- to the effect that McLay was eecidedly sober ori the day in question, and corroborated Mr. Jenkins's statements with regard to the prisoner's part in the subscription.—James Isherwood James Hamilton' and William Coins gave similar evidence. Mc Lay gave evidence to the effect that Ile was on picket duty early on tho morning in question and did. not taste any food for 14 hews. Consequently he was very weak. He had one pint of beer during that time and another at noon. Later in the day he had a third pint. On his oath he was not drunk, and denied challenging the Chief Constable to fight. —The Bench imposed a fine of 10s. including costs. The charge of assault was then proceeded with. Mr. Browne prosecuted end Mr. Jenkins defended. Mr. Browne said McLey was formerly employed at Messrs. Whyman's. Winstanley was employed there as

a bricksetter and slater. and had nothing to do with the strike dispute whatever. On Saturday morning it was found that some bricks had been taken out of a wall surrounding the works for the purpose of the men on picket duty climbing up to hok over ani see wtat was taking place in the works. Mr. %Wyman instructed Winstanley to put the bricks in again. A step ladder was then noticed against the works wall, and Mr. Whyman gave instructions to Winstanley to bring it away, but a ' woman claimed it as hers. Mr. %%layman proceeded to cross the abutment near Owen-street to go to the bank.. He had not gone many yards when .McLay struck Winstanley in the numth with his fist and tnocked three teeth loose. Winstanley reeled and was again struck at tie back of the head, causing pain, which he still felt. He whistled Mr. Whynaan hack, and .the latter found Winstanly bleeding from the month. These men had had a warning. and Mr. Jenkins gave an undertaking that they would behave then-selves. Messrs. Wly men were determined to pro- tect their men and to carry on their business in their own way. McLay could get £2 ss, when working for %Wyman's, and the difference, the proposed reduction would make to him was 70. a week. The way the men Were carrying en was lecoming unbearable to Why- man's, and he was instructed to press the case as he could. Winstanley was called and corroborated Mr. Brown's statements as to the assault, he had never had words with the prisoner before. Mr. Whyrnan also gave evidence. Mr. Jenkins for the defence, said McLay was on picket duty in Owen-street when he saw the ladder belonging to an old lady against the wall. Winstanley charged McLay with putting the ladder against the wall, at the same time using bad language. McLay was annoyed because he did not put the steps there. The steps tad been borrowed from an old lady in order to reach a kite, that was on %Wyman's wall. There was no intention of the men rising the steps for looking into the woks. Words ensued, and the men grabbed each other, and both fell to the ground. That was the whole of the assault, and if Winstanley knocked his mouth against the pavement McLay would no doubt regret it. iVinstanley brought it en himself.

McLay gave evidence in support of this statement. The Chairman said the Bench %ore satisfied the assault was committed, and McLay would be fined 20s. includ- ing costs.. EXTENSIVE PROVENDER THEFTS. - FARMER FINED FOR RECEIVING.

SCENE AT THE POLICE COURT.

Solicitor and Chief Constable on the Right of Entry. The question as to the right of entry to the Police Station caused (Mite a remarkable scene in the Police Court on Monday morning, wben Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins addiessed the Bench thus: I have an application to make. I have a number of witnesses who have been refused admission to the court.

The Chief Cotrstable : That is radically wrong. Mr. Jenkins is misrepresenting the affair all ogether. ?Jr. Jeukins.—l sincerely trust that my conscience will not pelmit we to address 3ou with a falsehood. The Chief Constable has branded me as a liar. It is not true. lam not a liar. When I approached the constable at the door I approached him politely, and explained to him that n number of the men who were waiting were witnesses, and he told me they would not be admitted. I asked the officer would he kindly allow them to follow me upyhecause I might possibly wish to speak to them in a room adjoining, which was specially for witnesses, but the police in their arrogance denied the right of the witnesses to come into the building. I am speaking the truth, and I say the police have no such right, and should not dream of exercising any such power. This is a Court of the Kinr, to which every subject is entitled to be admitted if he conductsdbirn- self with propriety. I deny the right of any pllice officer or of the Chief Constable, to dare to prevent a slbject of his Majesty, who conducts himself with propriety, entering the building. The Chairman (Mr. H. Roberts) : Do I understand that the officer in charge refus3d you personally 2 Mr. Jenkins: Yes, he pushed them all out. The Chief Constable Mr. Jenkins is apparently suffering under a misapprehension. I have asked him to give me the names of his witnesses. Your Worships know perfectly well thltt the police cannot keep witnesses out. It is quite a myth for him to mrke that statement. It is far-fetched.

The Chairman : Mr. Jenkins has stated that they were refused admission in his presence. The Chief Constable said that Mr. Jenkins bad 20 or 30 witnesses and without he supplied their names the police could not let them in. Mr. Jenkins : I submit that neither the. 'Chief Constable nor anyone else Las any right to inquire x,ho is a witness and who i 4 not. The public have a perfect right of admission to this Court, but if it becomes inconveniently crowded it is in the power of the magistrates to direct the Chief Constable to do what you_say._

The Chairman entirely agreed with Mr. Jenkins that the public had a right to the Court, and the police had no right to exclude them, except on grounds of public convenience, or possibly if the Chief Constable was of opinion that there might be some unseemly conduct in connection with any case.

Mr. Jenk:ns said he thought tho Bench would agree with him that, when ho courteously explained to . the officer that thtse men were his witnesses, and asked him to allow them to follow him into Court, so that he could speak to them he should have done so. - The officer ought to have allowed the iron in. He (Mr. Jenkins) declined most tespectfully to Le under,the dictation of nny official. The magistrates he v-ni-ottld obey with pleasure. He had been trained to do so, but he would not obey the Chief Coustable or any of his officers.

The Chairman asked ihe Chief Constable if, in exer- cislng his diEcretion, he had a suspicion of unseemly conduct?

The Chief Constable : Undoubtedly, and there were sefficieut people outside atm ,wanted to get into the Court who would have filled it half a dozen times over. The offi, er was directed not to allow them to come in until they were told—in the interests of the witnesses themselve.. There would not have been sufficient room for a quarter of them. Mr. Jenkins: I contradict that, unless lam a blind man.

Mr. Longton said Le also liad a difficulty in getting into the Court himself and &so in getting two defen- dants in.

The Chairman coukl quite understand the Chief Constable, in the interests of public decency nnd order, and in view of there being a strong feeling with regard to any particular case other than as members of the public. He Ind no doubt whatever, if the constable in question had acted somewhat art itrarily, it was from some misunderstanding on his part as to what his duties were. They would get over this if Mr. Jenkins would furnish the Chief Constable with the names of his u if,negses.

R. T. Fairclough, G. Dkin, John Fairclough, Dutton Faircleugh, P. Kirkham, NV. Limb, NV Lengbottom, A. Lowe, J. H. Plevin and W. Tinsley.— Messrs. D. Bankes and J. Lowe were re-appointed lay delegates, and Mr. Longbottom, auditor.— Votes of thanks were accorded to the officers, choir and

The Rev. H. Woods Perris was on a visit to Werring- ton last week-end and occupied his old pulpit at Cairo street Unitarian Chapel on Stineay. In the morning he preached with acceptance on " Three score and ten." A good congregation listened to him in the evening v hen ho discoursed upon" The art of life." He reminded them of Him who said" I am come that ye might have life and have it more abundantly." Lifo was a many sided affair, wondrously intricate, touching things that were low, and things that were high,—so low and so high that it eluded our search. That voice from Galilee taught awn to measure their.ratures by a higher scale of value than the market. Life contained all that WEIS ' best and wisest—politicians, seers,apostles, and art. Art was surely the satisfying embodiment of the zest of life. The secret of art vas joy, and the passion for true joy. A few of them found time for such stirring ascensions of spirit. A few found the impulse in their hearts and gave it full play. Very early In the world's history, we found select souls began to image their wonderful thoughts in clay-, etc. The old Bible reflected such things, and same of them were very poetic. 'Art in its true sense took in everything that ministered to the hungry, art brought out of the dust even the things in•-tinctive cud necessitous and they were gradually transfigured into the ideal forms which dignified human life. There 'had been a great deal of confueion as to the right use of the highest function of art. It could not be made a sub- stitute for religion, nor could relieion afford to set art aside, which the Puritans did. Great masters of art had tiied to mingle the earthly and the heavenly. They. had seen the ideal in the real and had mingled them, Culture must begin carly if it were to affect the character vholesomely. Although art was far from being mere imitation, the right way of (I,,ing things was better learnt by the close study of examples than by the special cram of later years. The way of doing or seeirg a thing was of prime importance. Slovenly writing, rude talking, and things vhich the eye did not care to ace, or the car to hear,should not be imitated. Toilers end accumulators said they must live. Of course they must live, but they ought to• resolve to truly live. The artistic sense was latent ill ninny who had never found they had that sense. Love of the beautiful in almost any form must he as a door into that spiritual vision. • The art of life w,'s the pur- suit of the highest, as life itself was. In conclusion he appealed to them to surrender their being in conscious service to the Will of the Highest, and they would be able to serve human life.

The preacher said he should like to recognize the kindly brotherly fooling on the part of their minister (the Rev. C. Harvey Cook) for allowing him the privi- lege of occupying the place that day and addressing some few whom he remembered many years ago, yamt he was Minister in that place. He trusted that till illness in Mr. Cook's family would soon pass away and that he would be able u ith strength and courage to resume his work among the people.

Leach) then gave his report, in the course of which he said th a although they had not had large attendances during the past seision, they had had meet- ings which were very helpful and calculated to da a great amount of good amongst young people. Owing to unorthodox lines being adopted progress had been some- what retarded. However, there seemei to ho a new spirit in the Guild movement generally and Bold-street was looking forward to the next session being one of the most successful in the history of - the movement. He was very sorry that owing to pressure of other work he found it neces- sary to resign the office of general secretary. Mrs. Pattison had promised to be his successor. Cairo-Street Unitarian Chapel: Visit of a Former Pastor.

A Primrose Day alarm and its Sequel

Terrified into a serious Nervious Disorder, this young woman found a complete cure in Dr. NVilliams Pink Pills.

Primrose day, in the memory of Aliss Kate Smart, of 50, Bight Street, Great Iledvqn, will always be assc- ciated with a thrilling experience that befel her in 1905. At that time, Miss Smart, whilo gathering primroses la a wood, wits terrified by a ferocious dog. "I shall never for- get how it upset me," she explained to a reporter recently. "A. few days Lter the- effects of the nervens shock became notice- able, for I could- not hold anything in my hands. MISSING BALLOONIST.

MONS. BELLAMY.

It is now feared that Mons. Bellamy, the balloonist, who made an Ascent from the Crystal Palace on Easter Monday, has been drowned in the North Sc?. No word has been received of the descent of the balloon on the Continent, and ever/ thing points to the fact that the hallow-, with its ill-fated occupant, must have dropped while crossing.

DEATH OF A WESLEYAN EX-PRESIDENT.

We regret to announce the death, which occurred at Brixton on Saturday, of the Rev. Dr. J. H. Rigg, who was twice President of the Wesleyan Conference. Dr. Rigg was born at Newcastle•on-Tyne on January 16. b. 1821. His father 91 al the Rev. John Rigg, a dis- tinguished Wesleyan minister, who " travelled " in some twenty circuits. His son James was educated at the Old Kingswood School, Bath, and afterwards became one of its masters ; later be was recognised as a local preacher. In 1845 he was accepted as "an itinerant minister on probation," and Sheffield was the first circuit to which he was sent. In 1856 he became well known in Lancashire through his appointment to the Stockport circuit, and still more when, in 1859, he removed to Manchester, where he remained for five yenra, first in connection with the liwell-street circuit and later with the Great Bridgewater-street circuit. Mrs. Riggs health compelled him to leave Lancashire for the South of England, „and so from 1864 to 1867 he laboured at, Folkestone, which he left for Stoke Newington where his ministry proved very short, as, awing to the death of t6e Rev. John Scott, he was .appointed Principal of Westminster Training College, a ppsition he retained until 1903. In 1878 Dr. Rigg was elected president of 1 the British Conference, having been from 1866 a mem- ber of the Legal Hundred. His presidency proved to be memorable, for it was marked not only by the admiss,on of laymen into the Conference, but else by the estab- lishment of a Thanksgiving Fund, by which a sum of over £300,000 was r.,ised. In 1892 he was for a se3ond time elected President of the Western Conference,and the aelecti,ln was welcomed even by many who did' not fIS a rule, favour second elections to the chair.—Dr. Rigg wrote a large number of pamphlets on religions and eduaational subjects,nnd was a frequent contributor to current periodicals.

The youth was working in the pit in connection with the haulage, when he was knocked down and severely injured about the legs. WARRINGTON I • CHAPTERS.

THE BIRTH-PLACE OF THE TEMPERANCE MOVEMENT.

On Wednesday next Mrs. Dodds will unveil a Memorial in the old Independent Methodist Schoolroom, Chapel Lane; Stockton Heath, which bears the inscription

IN THIS BUILDING on April the 4th 1830 THE FIRST ENGLISH TOTAL ABSTINENCE SOCIETY

was formed under the advocacy of George Harrison Birkett of Dublin, William Wood of Manchester, Thomas Eaton and William Clarke of Stockton Heath.

The building thus memorialized is a small and unimpressive one as public buildings go, but it has won a place among the world's shrines inasmuch as it is the undoubted birth-place of the modern Temperance Move- ment. The chain of circumstances which led to the event furnish materials for a Warrington Chapter of no small importance. TH4 FIRST BOOK ON TEMPERANCE. To what can the Temperance Movement be definitely traced? Who was it who sowed the first seeds of reform and who "set the thoughtful thinking " ? We think that the honour belongs to Basil Montague, a Barrister at Law, who in 1814 published a most thoughtful and earnest volume upon the subject of intoxicants. Its title was "Some enquiries into the effects of Fer- mented Liquors"and the fact that it was pub- lished anonymously suggests that its learned author was by no means sure that it would meet with favour. A second edition appeared in 1818 and a third followed it, but it was not until 1843 that it was acknowledged as the work of Basil Montague. Its 360 pages are packed with fact and argument, and every chapter has the note of moral passion. An interesting feature is the appeal which the author makes to good men to take up Temperance as a serious study and as a plank in the Church's programme. Though not a Quaker himself he goes out of his way to ask the Society of Friends to add temperance reform to their long list of noble and philanthropic efforts. It seems almost certain that it was from this appeal that the Temperance movement on this side of the Atlantic came into being. America had led the way in 1826 with Temperance Societies based upon an anti- spirit pledge, and these had been followed by several, first in Ireland, then Scotland and Yorkshire. We are concerned, however, not with Moderation Societies which allowed the lrinking of wine and beer, but with the Total Abstinence movement. GEORGE HARRISON BIRKETT.

The Apostle of Total Abstinence was George Harrison Birkett of Dublin. That several thoughtful men in the Irish capital bad given consideration to the problem is shown in a tract published by Dr. Urwick, a Congregational minister there in 1829, who boldly declared that the only remedy was "total abstinence from all intoxicating liquors." It was left to George Harrison Birkett to make the courageous experiment )f drafting and signing a total abstinence pledge, and calling upon all good men to join him in a crusade against intoxicants. His manifesto was addressed "To the inhabi- tants of Warrington," and it was here that he made his first public appeal and achieved his first success.

The Temperance Apostle was a well-known Dublin merchant and a member of the Society of Friends. Whether Basil Montague 's appeal to Quakers had first stirred him to thoughtfulness we do not know, but his writing suggests that he was familiar with his arguments. Far in advance of his gener- ation he had long been a vegetarian and had earned a nickname in consequence. It was not to be wondered that he showed unusual characteristics for he belonged to a family of marked originality. His father was William Birkett of Liverpool who in 1774 married Sarah Harrison of Kendal. They migrated to Dublin but always kept a close connection with the Worsdells of Warrington. J. W. Worsdell who died last year remembered their visits and wrote to us a year or two ago: "My grandmother was sister to Mrs. Birkett and the two sisters were women of strong and original mind. I well remember that Sarah Birkett was desirous of purchasing a new dress and my mother went with her to the best shop in the town near Market Gate, but Sarah dis- played such utter scorn of the goods shown to her that her sister, who, of course, was well-known in the town, was glad to get out of the shop. Whether any purchase was rands I do not remember but she tossed aside the things saying 'I would not give such a goon to the girl that milks my coo." • "William Birkett wore a very stiff hat with broad brim and he made a point, when retir- ing for the night, of putting on his hat and going upstairs, and in the morning he came down with his hat on, so we children wondered whether he slept with his hat on, but we could never find courage to ask him." With such an ancestry it was to be expected that George Harrison Birkett would show a courage that was not easily daunted, and a fine disregard for convention- alities. He was free however from eccentri- city and his fine education made him a worthy and able advocate of the cause he espoused. In the early part of 1830 he was residing with his relatives in Warrington having entered upon some business which brought him to England and in the month of March issued his,now-famous Manifesto.

===== 0002601_19090424_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WARRINGTON MANIFESTO.

It is headed "A few Arguments in favour of Temperance Societies" and the arguments centre round several propositions. The ' first is the Religious in which he makes appeal to scripture and conscience. The second is Medical and urges that temperance would abolish disease and promote health. The next note is Longevity and Strength and he passes from this to the Economic aspects of the drink evil as it affects the individual. The National aspect follows and deals with crime and the cost to the nation of the great evil of drink.

In closing he puts forward the pledge which he thinks all good citizens should sign : " We, whose names are subscribed, believing that intemperance with its attendant evils is pro- moted by prevailing opinions and practices with respect to the use of intoxicating liquors, and that decided means of reformation are loudly called for, resolve to abstain from the use of inebriating liquors ourselves, to dissuade others from using them and by all proper means to , discountenance the causes and practices of intemperance." This appeal was issued freely in the town and the next step was an endeavour to hold a public meeting in support of it. But the effort failed for want of a place of meeting. The town recoiled from a proposal so start- ling. The young Quaker was assured that beer was a necessity of life. To this he issued his counterblast: "A man has no more need of intoxicating liquors than of arsenic or

opium." But a place of meeting for such advocacy was not to be had at any price. The people of Friars' Green Chapel, after- wards the heroes of the movement, were yet to be convinced and did not feel able to open the building for such revolutionary propaganda. At Stockton Heath there were at least two men who were far more advanced. In the cottage of William Clarke, a Wesleyan local preacher, there was already a junior temper- ance meeting being held at which he and Thomas Eaton strove. to counteract the influence of the Wilderspool Brewery. Here was the very soil for which Birkett was wait- ing, and along with a friend of his, William Wood of Manchester, he proceeded to make a canvass of the village. The public meeting was held on April 4th, 1830, in the Independent Methodist Chapel and at its close the first society was formed. It is to Miss Elizabeth Clarke, a daughter of Wm. Clarke, who lived until last year that we owe the only detailed account we have of this memorable meeting. She signed the Roll Book at its close and kept in her memory a clear remembrance of the events of the evening. Better still, she preserved upon the death of her father, the papers and Roll Book in which the signatures were made, and these have enabled us to make good the claim that Stockton Heath was the birth- place of the Total Abstinence Movement. Manchester was the next sphere of operation and George Harrison Birkett and William Wood formed the second society there in the following month. Of this, and how Joseph Livesey and the men of Preston first heard of the Movement from this district, and how they afterwards took it up, we may tell another day. A.M.

===== 0002601_19090424_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR MISCELLANY.

THE ADULT SCHOOL.

We were-a—ble on Easter Sunday morning to make the acquaintance of the Warrington Adult School. After fifteeff months in temporary and inadequate premises the school is now comfortably housed in Academy- street and shows encouraging signs of growth and progression. Warrington has lagged far behind in the matter of Adult School work and we are glad that the movement has at last taken firm root. Here as elsewhere members of the Society of Friends have been actively interested and the school owes much to ihe zeal of Mr. Ransome.

-* * * *

The Easter holidays had told slightly upon the attendance but 47 were preSent,and that at the early hour of 8.45 may be con- sidered a very satisfactory gathering. The members, however, are far from satisfied, and propose to at least treble the member- ship during coming months. For ourselves we think the meetings have but to become known to become increasingly popular, and if our reference here induces anyone to rise a little- earlier on Sunday next and put in an attendance we shall feel that we have helped someone to a means of happinesi and grace.

* a * aIZ

The plan of meeting has much to do with its attractiveness. After opening exercises an address—lecturette is the correct Adult School word I think—occupies attention for 20 minutes. After this the Bible-lesson is opened by a member of the School followed by free discussion. The free democratic atmosphere is one in which men will feel quickly and perfectly at home and we shall not be surprised if the School becomes a force to be reckoned with in the town.

** * *

The scope of the lecturettes is unlimited. Social and economic subjects figure in the list, and though there are many who would prefer their exclusion it is recognised that the men have questions to ask on social topics, and benefit by the discussion of them in a religious atmosphere. It must be remembered, too, that the school is not theological college nor a literary institution, but a brotherhood, and its discussions must be as broad as its purpose. The school might well take as its motto an adaptation of a famous Latin saying: We are men, therefore all that concerns men—whether social or physical, economic or religious— concerns us.

** * *

It has various side activities such as a Savings Bank, a book club, a Social Com- mittee and recently a women's school has begun to meet on Sunday afternoon under the care of Mrs. Ransome. We were most favourably impressed by the syllabus for Bible study. In this depart- ment all the Adult -Schools follow the same programme and the monthly organ of the movement, "One and All," gives notes and helps. The plan of study gives variety with thoroughness and is the result of careful thought and choice. The schools appear to apply themselves to their study of the Bible, untrammelled by hoary tradition, and in the free discussion of the subjects the school marks a distinct advance upon the Pleasant Sunday Afternoon.

** * *

"Thoroughly Democratic" is a claim made for the School and the claim is made good by the method of government. To quote ah official circular, the Adult School is a place where:

"A man's a,man for a' that," Without a collar, tie, or hat, Or eren a coat, for matter o' that If but his heart is beating true A man's a man, no matter who! ** * *

FIFTY YEARS AGO.

- - April 22nd, 1859. On this day the restoration of the venerable Parish Church was formally commenced by the laying of the foundation stone. A procession was formed at the National Schools headed by the Blue Coat School Band, and by the time it reached the Church the venerable building was almost crowded in every part. -Prayers were read by the Rector and the Bishop of Chester preached an excellent sermon from the words " Except the Lord build the house their labour is but lost that build it." At the conclusion of the service the people collected round the foundation stone—a block of red sandstone from Mr. Lyon's quarry at Hill Cliffe, from which all the stone required was taken without charge.

** * *

In front of the stone a platform to accom- modate 300 ladies was erected and filled at once. • Among others were Mrs. Queckett, Mrs. Gilbert Greenall, Mrs. Greenall (of Stretton), the Misses Wilson-Patten, and many others. The stone was laid by the Rector (Rev. William Queckett).

In accordance with the usual custom several articles were placed under the stone, viz: A photograph of the Parish Church, by the Rector, the " Times " and the "Standard," a collecting book, copies of the following: "Rambles in Old Warrington" by Dr. Kendrick, the Rector's lecture on "The history of a Letter," the Mayor's lecture on "The Moon," the Rector's account of the trip to London taken by 400 of his parishioners, and photographs of the Rector, Colonel Wilson Patten, John Clare, Esq., and , several current coins of the realm.

MY LIFE STORY.

COLOUR SERGEANT HENRY BOND BOWERS.

Although it is now forty years since I left the army, military service claimed such a large share of my earlier days and I had the honour of taking part in such momentous engagements during the Persian war and Indian Mutiny, that my life story will pro- bably be of interest to your readers. I was born on August Bth, 1830, at the small town of Tewkesbury in Gloucestershire. My father was a stocking maker by trade and died when I was but nine years of age, and my mother passed away when I was fifteen. Although I do not come of a military stock, it is worthy of note that a great uncle of mine fought at Waterloo where his horse was shot from under him and the upper part of his helmet was carried away by a bullet. I had very little education but learned to read fairly well at the Church of England Sunday School. At the age of nine, I began work as a winder for stocking-makers and ultimately became a stocking maker. Leaving that work, I served as a grocer's errand lad for a time, and afterwards worked in a tan yard and also as a bricklayer's labourer. I determined, however, to join the army and on October 24th, 1848, enlisted in the 64th Regiment, now the Ist North Staffordshire Regiment. Being drafted to Ireland I joined the regiment at Clonmel on November 17th and at the end of December we marched from there to Cork where we embarked for India on January 9th, 1849. Arriving there in May we were successively stationed at Poona, Kurrachee, and Heidara- bad. In February 1852 we returned to Kurrachee where I was troubled with fever and ague for thirteen months. In December 1852 we were sent home for a change of climate and for upwards of twelve months we were quartered at Chatham. I was married on May 21st, 1854 and when the call came to return to India in September of that year my wife accompanied me. The regiment was stationed at Belgaum until the end of October, 1856 when on the Persian war breaking out we were sent to that land. Our first engagement,under General Stalker's command, was the storming of Fort Reshire on December 9th, 1856. It was a difficult operation, the rocks which encircled the fort having to be scaled, and the colonel of our regiment, Colonel Stopford, was the first man to fall. However, we succeeded in taking Lthe fort and next day we stormed Fort Bushire, another important position. The following year General Sir J. Oultram and General Sir H. Havelock took charge of our division and under their leadership we fought the battle of Koosh-ab on February 2nd, 1857 and took the batteries of Mohammerah on March 26th, 1857. The latter was the decisive engagement and imme- diately afterwards peace was declared. lOne incident during the Persian campaign illustrates the tactical skill and daring of General Outram. Dressed as a Persian he went amongst the natives and was able to save our column from disaster, for he learned that had we traversed a certain pass as originally contemplated we should certainly have been annihiliated. As it was, we were able to go by another route in safety. Scarcely had the Persian campaign con- cluded when news of the terrible mutiny in India came to hand and we were destined to play an prominent_part in quelling it. We embarked from Bushire for Bombay on May 7th, 1857, and on landing in India we were immediately sent up to Calcutta and thence to Aliahabad, under the command of General Havelock. We formed part of " Havelock's little band" which gained for itself a worthy place in the pages of history. It would take an elbquent pen to describe all that we passed through, and all I will relate are a few unvarnished facts. It was a trying time for us all, but the honour of having done some service to our country in a critical situation was sufficient reward. As for myself, I am thankful to say that despite the many dangers encountered, I came through practically scathless aud to the present day have enjoyed robust health. Along with the 78th Regiment we first attacked the mutineers at Futtehpore on the 12th July. The forces arrayed against us were almost two to one, for we had only 1,800 men and eight guns to match against their 3,500 men and twelve guns. The Sepoy mutineers occupied ground ,broken by swamps, groves, and hillocks as a front line, with the enclosures of Futtehpore to fall back upon, but General Havelock exer- cised great skill in the disposition of the forces at his disposal, and the enemy were completely routed. Havelock issued a stirring order of the day in which he attributed the victory "to the power of the Enfiffii rifles in British hands, to British pluck, that great quality which has survived the vicissitudes of the hour, and gained intensity from the crisis; and to the bless- ing of Almighty God on a most righteous cause, the cause of justice, humanity, truth and the good government of India." Remark- able to state, we did not lose a single nian in the engagement, but numbers died from sunstroke. Eleven of the enemy's guns fell into our hands. Having rested on the 13th, the march was resumed, and .at daybreak on the 15th we again found ourselves confronted

b--ithe enemy-. Having taken the village of Aong we pushed on as quickly as possible to ' the rebels' main position at Pandoo Nuddee. Two hours marching under a burning sun—and that after having been on foot since midnight--brought us to the bank of the river. The Sepoys were arrayed in strong force beyond the bridge but a per- sistent fire eventually dislodged them, the bridge was carried with a rush, and the mutineers took to their heels and made off for Cawnßore. We lost twenty-five killed and wounded and took four guns. On we pressed to Cawnpore. For twenty- four miles we had to march along an arid road, the scorching sun glaring pitilessly upon us. i. When news was brought by a

spy that the women and children who were held prisoners ,by Nanah Sahib at Cawnpore had been massacred, a thrill of horror went through the ranks. There was a three hours

halt for rest and refreshment, and some porter which had been left by the natives in a ditch by the road side was distributed to the troops, in order to stimulate them for the unusual labours that lay before them. How- ever, to cut a long story short we encountered the enemy outside Cawnpore and after a terrible struggle put them to flight. General Havelock gallantly led his forces, exposed to the full view of the enemy, and his horse was shot from under him. Our regiment played a cclnspicueus part in the rout and General Havelock himself in describing the final stage of the battle wrote: "The enemy sent round shot into our ranks until we were within 300 yards and then-poured in grape with such precision and determina- tion as I have seldom witnessed. But the 64th, led by Major Stirling and by my aide- de-camp who had placed himself in the front, were not to be denied. The rear showed the ground strewed with wounded but on they steadily and silently came; then, with- a cheer, charged and captured the unwieldly trophy of their valour. The enemy lost all heart, and after a hurried fire of musketry gave way in total rout." The num- ber of British who fell in the battle was 105. The aide-de-camp referred to was Gen. Havelock's own son, Henry Havelock, who gained the Victoria Cross for his work during the mutiny. And well he deserved it, for he was a man who never knew what danger was. I myself have seen him ride up within pistol shot of the enemy to reconnoitre. Unfortunately he met his death whilst on a visit to Afghanistan in comparatively recent years. On July 20th, after their capture of Cawnpore, Gen. Have- lock issued an order thanking the troops, in which he stated: "64th. You have put to silence the gibes of your General's evil- wishers throughout India by reserving your fire until you saw the colour of your enemy's moustache." On entering Cawnpore, traces of the terrible massacre which shocked all England were only too distinct. The house in which the slaughter of the women had been perpetrated was literally steeped in blood, and flesh and hair were adhering to the walls. The bodies of the victims had been flung into an adjacent well.

Leaving Cawnpore we crossed the Ganges and entered the Oude country, taking part in four engagements to entice the tribes away from the residency of Lucknow, which was now under siege. On July 29th, 1857, we captured Oonao and Busseetit Gunge and nineteen guns. This position we were obliged to give up, but retook it on the 15th of August. The battle of Borea-Ki-Choki followed on August 12th. From October 7th to November 16th our regiment was engaged in the defence of the Alumbagh under Colonel Mclntire, of the 78th Highlanders. On 17th November, 1857, we took part in the relief of Lucknow, headed by General Sir Colin Campbell, and you can well imagine what a welcome we received from those who had been so anxiously await- ing our arrival. The final engagement in which we fought was the battle of Kallee Nuddeo which took place on January 2nd, 1858. Your readers will probably be interested in some lines on the 64th Regiment, written by one of its members, an Irishman. Some Warrington people will recollect that I recited them at the old folk's treat twelve months last January.

The gallant General Havelock, whO had gained such glory in the campaign, was destined not to return home. His health had been undermined by the cares and anxieties he had undergone and an attack sf dysentery put an end to his brilliant career on November 21st, 1857. I was one who helped to wrap the horse cloth round the departed general prior to his burial. During tie whole of the mutiny my wife, and two daughters who were born in India, were locked up in the fort at Belgaum along with many other women and children, and I have heard my wife say how she looked after the children while other women loaded-the rifles for the soldiers. For two years and a half we had not the opportunity of seeing each other and we were devoutly thankful when we were safely re-united. I witnessed many narrow escapes ehiring the mutiny, but one of the most remarkable was when a colour-sergeant was fired at and was saved by the bullet striking some coins in his pocket. Our mutiny engagements over, the regi- ment returned to quarters at Kurrachee and we were quartered there for two-and-a-half years. On April 10th, 1861, we embarked for home, and arrived at Dover on August 6th. After being at various stations in England we set sail for Malta on February sth, 1867. Returning from there on Decem- ber 18th, 1868, I took my first discharge on January 19th, 1869, after service which counted 21 years and three months. I have therefore been drawing a pension for just forty years. For three years and six months I served in the 4th Royal Lancashire Militia and was stationed at Warrington, first in the old Cockhedge Barracks and afterwards in the barracks which stood on the site of the present Police Station.

I was the champion shot of the 45th Regiment in Malta and took the first prize for shooting four times in the 4th Royal Lancashire Militia. As to my civilian occupa- tions, I was for four years with Mr. Charles Burgess grocer, Bridge-street,and for twenty- one years with Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons Ltd. For the past twelve years I have lived in retirement. My proudest possessions are a medal and clasp for the Persian war, medal and clasp for, the relief of Lucknow, and medal for long service and good conduct.

CHAPTER XVIII. (Continued.)

(In which we hear something of the Pagcank at Lowestoft.]

This badinage was a pretty prelude to what was to come. We were up early next morning, and the streets all a-blowing and a-growing before the fishing boats had come in. You do not know Lowestoft, Paddy, but you will please to understand that there is, beneath the northern cliff, a very pretty stretch of grass land, whereon golfers and others disport themselves. Toward this at eleven o'clock in the morning, half the in- habitants and all the visitors wended their way • and here the great battle between the invading Danes and the inhospitable Britons was to take place, directed by my cousin Arthur, who- carried a megaphone and took his stsition upon a watch-tower. As this is not a horsey locality, the most pert of us went afoot, and a fine, straggling show of hire-purchase assassins we must have looked to any sane man who had happened upon us at hazard.

Just imagine' the narrow, fishy street of a fishy town packed from end to end with twentieth-century monks, romping British maidens, Druid priests, Danes, and Norse- men. Depict a fat grocer waddling along in a tin-pot helmet and a tunic down to his knees. Create for yourself the image of a substantial matron in a frock that looks like a—but you have seen the catalogues of the linen houses and will spare my blushes. Over all wave the flags and the banners. There are a few horsemen—six-and-six the first hour, six shillings afterwards--a great many battle-axes, pikes, and spiked maces. The fishermen, who stare as we pass by, laugh vulgarly. Some of the youths, who are to do police to separate them. 1 observe that the the fighting, begin already and need the girls are all in a hurry to be carried scream- ing to the hills and that they rehearse their parts upon any opportunity, even the most trifling. Such was the beginning of thp Pageant at Lowestoft. Never, I suppose, was Arthur Warrington in better form; it was better than any Criterion farce to hear him shout- ing the stage directions from his watch- tower. Of course, had he been a clever man, he would have engaged some genius from the theatres to have helped him as to the stage management; but he is not a clever, man, and chaos followed him to the battle-field. Oh, my dear Paddy, what a joy it was when that bellicose crowd heard him bawling: "Harps to the mound; all the lyres this side; an- cient Britons,. quick march; Danes ashore !" Could energy and a shrill voice have achieved success Arthur assuredly would have been crowned there and then with a laurel; but, alas, what are energy and shrill- ness when your Druid priest is invariably in the refreshment tent and your Danes upset the boat which is bringing them ashore? My dear Paddy, the hills would. *have rung with your laughter and the sea eveu it back to you in roaring harmonies. A dance of "early, British maidens," in the afternoon, left footprints on the sands of time; but the, attempt of a monk to join in the business, came near to ruining it. The Boat four:d us at last.

thering darkness, not a light in sight sa%r at .distant Lowestoft, the wind blowing cold as a blast from the hills, the sun gone down in a cloud, and the sea rising with a mournful cry which would have shamed the spirits of desolation. What to do, ho* to act even an old sailor might have been puzzled to say. My primitive maritime knowledge, Obtained upon the yachts at Trotiville, suggested an attempt to keep the head of the boat towards the swelling breakers and her bows above the crests. of the increasing waves. I at by Martha's side, and, employing the remaining scull as a paddle, tried to achieve so desir- able an end. But not without Many a weird evolution which came near- to costing Us our lives; .

To be candid, I was within an ace of drowning my couein's'pretty wife,- and that's the whole truth of it. If you would give me Len thousand pounds upon the table, 1 rould not again encounter those grim hours of help- less battling with Monstrous waves and •ia- creasing winds blowing upon us out of the void of the night. , Such a sense of loneliness and despair have never experienced. - We seemed to have left the world .of .men far behin4 us. Great hollows opened and threatened to engulf us. We mounted to crests and beheld a grey horizon capped by mountainous clouds with the moon struggling to break a golden way amid them. I knew then that long hours had passed; I doubted that we should ever see the shore again. And what does a woman do in moments like these. Well, if little Martha may speak for -her sex, she cries a little, laughs for con- trast, shivers when the cold can no longer be denied, and grows hot with. hope- upon the slightest word of encouragement. When I told her that I espied the -light of a . fishing- boat, she. put both her arms about my neck and kissed me—when I had to admit that it was sailing away to .the northward she just cried like a child who has met with disap- pointment. Nor was. that poor creature, her husband, often out of her thoughts. A woman's devotion to the man she lias married may be diverted by his own follies, but the right kind of woman goes back upon it in fife hour of danger. So with pretty Martha to- night. She wept not for herself but for the man's sorrow—and there I could not comfort her at all.

It would have been near 4 four o'clock of the morning and full light • when the boat they had sent- out from Lowestoft found us at last. We both got wet to the skin going aboard her, and were wrapped up in blankets when we arrived at the Vicarage. Shall I say that Arthur receiv,4l us with a tragic air? Nothing of the kimr; he just blubbered like a schoolgirl and was down on his marrow-bones —for which I honoured him—there and then. His demand for explanations came , after- wards. Those were tragic indeed. "There must be a public account of this," he • said. I told him not to be a fool, and he retorted by asking me to leave his house. ' do not --say," he was good enough. to re- mark, "that you can command the elements. Such was the power of the men of old. But at least you should know better than to leave the licach, in full view of the people, with my wife as your companion, in so mad a folly. For that I shall never forgive you." "Then you won't continue to say the Lord's prayer," was my retort—and I left him to think upon it. But, naturally, I couldn't stay here, Paddy —so where should I go but to Cromer, where "The Chimes " are playing. Be sure that the palms of these worthies were greased long ago, that the gentleman known as Jack eendall, has bought a new overcoat, that the lady at the piano is resplendent in a 'wonder- ful gown of satin, and that aromatic cigars of 4 Belgian brand are freely smoked by the company.

Mimi is now a queen among them. But I am daring to hope that her sovereignty will be transferred elsewhere very soon—and that my daring plan will be rewarded by that success which you, my dear Paddy, would be the first to wish me. So in high hope find me, Your friend, HARRY GASTONARD.

CHAPTER XIX.

[ln which we translate a letter from Henry Gastonard of the Maison du bon Tabae, at Hampstead, to Mimi the Simpleton, at Felixstowe.]

Mai Son du bon Tabac' HampsteadAugustAuust 29, 1905. Chere you remember when Mademoiselle Mamelle taught us to sing— "J'ai du bon tabac dans ma tabatiere, J'ai du bon •tabac, tu n'en aurais pas?" There is a song, ma mie, which others have been singing this afternoon in the house which their old comrade Mimi will not enter. Ali, mes enfants! And what shall I say to them.?

'Behold the builder, and ask if -he be not punished enough. So much is admitted by those who have climbed. upward to this height as you and I, ma mie, climbed upward in the. old days, by the Villa Policbinelle, under the sails of La Galette, to the little house where Gabriel de Math had, written immortality upon the windows and your old friend Desmond Barrymore used to sing you to -sleep while he painted. They came here, Mimi, out of the Paris

they love, to bring a message from the Butte to this savage band. But one i missing who should lia've welcomed them—ah, m en- fants.

It is not Paris this new house of the bon Tabac at Hampstead; but you might rub your eyes sometimes and believe another story. Here, as upon our own beloved Butte,- there stands the villa with. the roses :twined about it; here is the same bangle of a garden that served the Chevalier for his verses; here we sip the red wine and sing the .sengs which Jean Bataille taught us One yoice alone is missing. One who used to love us will not come to us—and the roses droop,, and silence falls, and we know that we have not forgot- ten.

• Riches did not build this hour, ma mie, nor Will they support it. We are all poor as in the splendid clays. If open a little window and look down to the valley, we sec the great city, and try to make believe that it is the Paris we love. Ah, what a cheat is that, and how willingly we consent to say, There is the dente of the Invalides, and there St. Jacques, and there. the frowsy houses of the Midi'. Georges Oleander, the pitiful old mendicant, started the game and is the busiest to play it—but your old friend Des- mond Barrymore makes one of the conspira- tors, and he has a little bust 'of you in clay upon the table as I write. • Not Paris, but to those who will that it shall be so, a city of their ,desires.. For what laud is not a home to us when 'Old friends are about is and there is good wine upon the table, and we may eat a Chateaulmand auz

CHAPTER XX.

[ln which Mimi ,replies. to Monsieur Henry. 11, The Parade, Felixstowe. Wednesday. •Ah, cher -Monsieur Henry, ii I knew .how to answer you.

Why should I go to dream a little while if I must awake to remember. Ah, mule noms, faut-il etre Parisienne. There must be roses in the heart if we would wear them on the cheek; but in my heart none, Monsieur Ifenry ; for it has grown empty. I hear the Chevalier—but why would he call me to that great city of shadows? Does Monsieur Earrymore laugh at me when he would have me believe that he is happy in this England? Shall I think well of Mon- sieur Oleander because he -also is deceived a little while? Here I look all day . across ,Lile sea where they tell me that France is. I am a child, but they call me a woman. Ah, mcs enfantS ! What pages I have turned in this great book of sorrow that none may see my tears fall upon them. A little while and the sun will shine, and then there is the great cold road again and the sad-faced people, and we go 'away, oh, so far away towards the dark and the night; and there is no light in the sky behind us to tell us where the city is ; we hear no Laughter anywhere ; but the end of the world is beyond, and we voyage with shut lips to- ward it.

I remember such a journey as this; it was long ago when 1 was so little, that you, blonsieur Henry, could have put me in your great . big pocket. An old woman led me by tM hand away from a. great - warm house down to the water and the ships. I re- member that it was twilight and then night, and that I saiv my home behind me as a star one sees low down in the heavens. And then we came to a hut in the wood, and there were ugly .men, and I • could not sleep, and when it was day all that I had known went from my mind, and I remembered nothing but the lonely road, and the strange 'faces, and the harsh words I heard. You know how far I have journeyed since those days. Al 4 what palaces we have visited to- gether, you and I, Monsieur Henry--,-and how often I have lost you! Can you wonder that I would rest—even I?

_ I dance still with these English friends, and they are very kind to me. The people here say that my dancing is wicked; but there are many clergymen, and they love to say • " shockink. ' , I live in a little room where I can watch the sca, and I go out every morning, long "before the people are up, to float on the waves,, and look for the ship 'which will carry me back to France. But there is no mermaid here, no fairy swim's with me; I cannot find the silver shell, and I return to my little house to say it will be never, that I shall see France no more, that all the world has deserted me.

Shall I make you sad, Monsieur Henry, to tell you all these things? If I do, the Che- valier will make you, laugh, and that will be the recompense. Oh, he is droll, the Cheva- lier. Do you remember when he loved Madame la: Comtess'3 de Brianville—and

would have borrowed twenty francs to marry her? Ah, mes enfants—but those were days!

And Monsieur Georges. Yes, 1 would like to see him again, and that great Monsieur Barrymore who used •to sit me on his knee before he painted my picture.

And they are all hi your Maison du bon Tabac, and there is Paris below the win- dows, the Paris you love to dream of, and you sing the songs that Jean de Bean) made. Ah, mes enfants—if I were there ! Your friend, (To be Continued.)

RIOT ON A FOOTBALL GROUND.

A serious riot took place on the Hampden football ground, Glasgow, on Saturday afternoon at the close of the Scottish Cup final. For the second week in succession the tie between the Celtic and Rangers' clubs ended in a draw, and the Crowd demanded that the teams should play extra time, but the authorities had made no arrangement for that. Thousands of spectators brae into the playing pitch and proceeded to tear up the goal posts. Mounted constables arrived, and in the melee that followed many persons were injured. The crowds then turned their attention to pay boxes and barricad- iogs, which were soon blazing. The fire brigade was summoned, but no sooner had they run out the hose than the crowd jumped on it and rendered the efforts of the firemen/ useltss. In the riot over fotty persona, mostly policemen were injured and treated at the infirmary, but, very few were detained, the majority suffering only from cuts and bruises caused by stones and bottles thrown by the mob. The damage to the enclosure amounts to hundreds of pounds.
