CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

EXHIBITION OF PRODUCTIONS AT THE PARR HALL.

The Parr Hall is this-week transformed into a hive of business activity, for the Warrington' Co-operative Soeitty enterpiisingly 'joined with the Co-opera- tive Wholl s:tle Society In fs.romoting. an exhibition in order to bring4be wide range of Co-operative produc- tions more prominently before the public. There are magnificent show cases 'stocked with tempting wares, ingenious nie .hani cal ccntrfitatices, sample stalls where tombs an,- edibles can be obtained, and a very credi- table collection of goods required • for domestic purposes. Cigars and cigarettes, blushes, sweets, soap and shirts are made while you wait, with the aid a electrically driven machinery, and the productions on view range • from biscuit s to fireirons, sui s and costumes to candlis and cocoa. Each evening a well known local band has been in • attendance and discomsed pleising stlettions latge and appreciative audiences. While admission to the hall is free, a charge of one penny each is made for the privilege of sitting in the •gallery during the musical entertainments, and all the money Vats o ceived is to be hat.ded over to the Infirmary and District Nursing A,•is ,ciation. It is interesting to note that the Co-operative Wholes tie Society, Ltd., whi as trade is £25,000,000 per annum, is a federation of 1,420 retail C,-operative Societies, aggregating 1,815,415 members, and representing about 7;000,000 consumers. - All the shares are held by tliPs...3 societies in proportion to their membership, and the profits are divided among the societies accord- ing to the atnounvf their purchases. OPENING BY TEIE MAYOR.

There etas a large attendance at the opening of the exhibition on Saturday afternoon by the Mayor (Alderman Forshae), who was accompanied by the Mayor. as. Mr. T. Wadsworth (president of the Society) presided, sod amongst others present were :—Mr. Willarn Laiide is(chairman of the Productive Committee of the C.W.S.) Mr. Ryan (rideertising department), Messrs. 141. Englend, G. T. Rushton, C. H. Hibhert, T. Critchley, 13. Farrar, W. Bihby,.R. Hall, N. Cook, J. Edge (local committee), J. Jarman (secretary), W. Jones and J. 11. Knowles (who took part in the formation of the Society), S. Albinsou (assistant secretary) and J. Crowther. The Cuairman, after apologising for the absence of Mr. Henry Roberts, who regretted that he was unable to preside, said the object of the exhibition was to draw the attention of the members of the Society to the productive side of the movement. It was the fire t time in the history of the Society that they had held such an exhibition in Warrington, and the Co-operative Wholesale Society, who were seeking to bring their business before the public, were producers of goods to the iimount, of £5,000,000 a year. That was a large amount-a arid naturally the Wholesale Society asked that membecs in making their purchases shoed give preference to the goods manufactured by them. They could rely not only upon getting good quality but upon the fact that the goods were produced under" the hest of conditions, so far es labour and the rate of wages were cencerned. The Co-operative movement, which know no creed or politics, had done more to elevate the working population of this country than any other movement of the present generation. They were pleased that the Mayor had no hesitation but at once complied with the request of the Education Coni- mit tee of the Society to open that exhibition. Alder- man Forshaw was a man who had made his way in the world and achieved the highest honour which his native town could c nfer upon him. During the past five years the local society had made extraordinary pro- gress but there was still room for development and they expec'ecl that when their new premises and other altera- tions were complete they would be able to supply all the demands of their mernberg.During the five past years the membership had increased from 7,000 to over 9,000. The sales five yea's ago amounted to ,£132,000 for the year; now they reached £190,000. Almost a seventh of that sum was paid hack to the memhers in dividend, which meant thet about £23,000 was distributed amongst the iehebitants of Warrington.. There was no doubt it must have done a great amount of good, especially amongst the working population. The capital of the Society, which was the capital of the members, was £76,000. (Applause). The MAYOI2, who had a cordial reception, said he waited them to understand Hirt he was there entirely in his civic capecav as the Mayor of Warrington. He bad come to the "determination that as the Mayor be would not represent any particular section of the people but that he would endeavour to represent the people generally. He thought it was as little as he could do to come and open that exhibition for the Co-operative Society, because when they were in great diAress in the town the Society sent a very handsome donation, and ,one good turn deserved another. (Applause). He understood the object of the exhibition ea.] that the Wholesale Society could show what they could manufacture. He remembered the local Co-operative Society being formed some forty-eight years ago by Warringtonians, one of whom was their very worthy townsmen Mr. Frederick Monks. (Applause.) It had'sprung from very small beginnings to a membership of over 9,000, and the progress of the Society was evidenced by tbe. commodious premises which were now being erected in Sankey-street. He was sure the Wholesale Society would be able to show them some splendid exhibits, aed in declaring the exhibition open he hoped it would prove a success. (Applause). - Mr. LANDER, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Mayor and the Chairman, said the fact that the Mayor was broad-minded and large-hearted enough to take the view he had (line and esiocat4 .himself with the people's movement by opening the exhibition showed that he was the right man in the right place. (Applause.) He (the speaker) had known Nlayors who took a narrow view of things and avoided dangerous paths such as that, and when he feund a men broad enceigh to recog- nize the claims of every class of the community he accorded him the meed of praise which was his due. The object of the exhibitio nwas that Co-operation as it was known through the medium of the local society should be strengthened and increased in the town and that As a means of bringing that about, the public shoted 1, now more of Co-operation in its distributive sense and also in its larger aspect as a wholesale factor and as a very potent influence in the development of the industrial reformation in this country. "flue local society was one of the links in the ch In of national and international Co-operation, which was binding men and women together for mutual advancement, for the betterment of the domestic,•intellectnal and social life of the, people, and the estrblishment of the busimeir and commercial life of the coontry upon a more collective basis than in the past: They believed as co-operators that ecollectivism was the system Of political- economy that was most suited to bring stout the greatest good for the greatest number in the community. The socialism of Co-opera- tion was practical in its work and Christian-like in its principlt s and dreams. They believed the present system of the distribution of the Wealth of the country was had end wrong. They were living in -days when poverty and unemployment were prominent questions and they said that whilst millionaires were made, the truest test of a nation's wealth was not the number of millionaires that the country coat a'ned hut the extent to nhich its wealth nes -made to minister to the peace,contentment and happiness of the greatest number in the community. Mr. Under sketched the rise elf the co-operative move- ment from its inception sixty-five years ago by twenty- eight workingmen of Rochdale.- To-day there were nearly 3,000,000 co-operators in the United Kingdom. The £2B of share capital had grown toover £10,000,000 a year, and there was coming back to the working. classes something like £12,000,000, t 3 £14,000,000 as the turnover was over £100,001,000 their share in the prJfile as the result of the Sawing of j the seed by those earnest men in Kochdale. (Applause). The Wholesale Society was a federation of 1420 retail societies. It was established to do the merchant work of the movement and had done it so successfully that Jo-day after forty-five years operations, the turnover was £25,600,000—ha1f a million per week. The Society came inta contact with growers and makers of geode in other lands and it was therefore exerting an international inflneuce in the way of peace. If they could only get m that bond of brotherhood between an and man,join- ing the astir as of the earth together' they would not need to talk about Dreadnaughts. (Applause). The workingclasses of the world did not want to fight one another,— not a bit of it. What they had to fight were the social difficulties of their own country and time. The great ideal and fundamental basis of the Co-opera- tive movement was the social and, industrial betterment of the people of this great Empire. There were now 32 factories and workshops owned by the Co-operative Wholesale Society and they had got so far in supplying the needs of-their members that they could almost begin at the cradle and end at the grave. In those works 11,000 people were employed. Over half a million pounds were paid in wanes last year and there was a gross profit of £300,000. They had a strong belief that the hours of labour and the wages of the worker could be improved and he believed personally that the day was not far distant when a 48 hours week could be adopted by the whole industrial world. The Co-operative Society had adopted it in their produc- tive works, and in some where female employment was predominant 441, hours were worked. The minimum wage they recognized for all male skilled labour over 21 years of agawas 245. per week. He (the speaker) did not believe that was enough. Any man was worth more than f he was worth anything at all. Because of the selfish- ness in human nature and the inconsistency and apathy of the individual, he was gradually coming to the belief that the improved position would have .to be brought about by the Government passing a Wages Bill to ensure that all trades should pay a fair wage and also fix the hours, as was already done in many industries. Though the productions of the Society amounted to aver £5,000,000 a year, there was no reason why—with the number of co-operators they had to-day, that sum should not easily he doubled or trebled in the near future. In conclusion Mr. Lander said they bad brought the exhibition to Warrington to show them that co-opera- lion was the goldou bridge by wbioh t4o working.'

PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES.

By Veronica.

THE PHOTOGRAPHIC COMPETITION.

A competition that has been organised in connection with the" Daily Mirror " Fair of Fashions to be held at Westminster in the last week of June, is one of novel, but yet of great interest. Prizes are being offered for photographs of subjects illustrating different periods of fashions of dress. This gives a very wide field to choose from. For instance, subjects of history may be depicted such as Mary, Queen of Scots; Joan of Arc, etc., or from literature, when characters from Shakespeare or the many charming Tennysonian heroines would •make equally suibable subjects. Competitions such as these are very helpful from an educational point of view, apart from the prizes that are attached to the competition. The latest date for the delivery of pictures is May 15th. Those who are interested in the matter can obtain particulars by writing to the Secretary of The Fair of Fashions, 12, Whitefriars-street, London, E.C.

FOLIAGE GROWTH.

With the advent of warmer weather the trees are quickly springing into leaf, and now is the time, uefore the trees are too heavily ladened with foliage, to obtain photographs, especially when photographing in sunlight when the shadows from tree-trunks ofte" help to break up the 'foreground much better than the shadows that would be thrown when the foliage was denser. •

===== 0002601_19090424_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
FILM PACKS,

The advantage of carrying film packs instead Of dark-slides is so obvious that it is not surprishel to find new applications of this method being plac on the market. There is the " Premopack" of the Kodak Co., and recently another pack, called 'Ensign "has been introduced. This has a very ingenious arrange- ment for drawing the different films. There is a series of spring catches somewhat similar to the catch usually fitted to dark-slides, and according to which film you wish to expose you withdraw the shutter whilst holding down the spring catch that is similarly numbered. The latest innovation hales from the, house of Lumiere, and is called the "Bloc-Film. This is for use in daylight with any camera fitted with an attachment .for film packs.

===== 0002601_19090424_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
APPROPRIATE TRAINING.

Many prints would be greatly improved by omitting certain portions of the picture, but many workers if they are using, say, a plate camera, invariably trial, and often mount their prints in an album all full size. In cases of slip-in mounts this cannot, very well, be obviated but in the case of a paste-down album it is a, great relief to find prints of unequa.l size instead of so much regularity of size. It frequently happen that a print is improved by trimming off some of the foreground, and not only the foreground, but at times the other parts as well. To see to what will be best to trim off it is a good plan to use strips of paper to cover up different parts of the print before attempt" ing to cut it.

===== 0002601_19090424_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
,FAILURES AND REMEDIES.

Common failures at the commencement of the season are incorrectness of exposure and development. If the plate has been overexposed the image flashes up quickly, and instead of using 'a little restrainer, the worb er sometimes feels that he may lose the picture altogether and so throws off the developer and fixes, with the result of getting a very thin negative, pro- ducing only a flat and very indifferent print.. Tho only way to deal with a negative of this description is to intensify it. On the other hand some make the mistake of carrying development too far, with the result that the negative is so dense that it takes a very long time to print, and any detail that should be io the lightest parts ts entirely lost. Negatives of this kind can frequently be saved by reducing. One of the simplest methods is the Fanner formula as follows: Add a few drops of 10 per cent. solution of ferri- cyanide of potassium to a solution of loz. of hypo dissolved in sozs. of water. Be careful not to add many drops, just sufficient to give a pale yellow colour to the solution, otherwise it will act too , quickly, and you will run the risk of spoiling your picture. In o few minutes the negative will gradually reduce ill density, and when you have this of the right printing quality, take it out and immediately wash for a coil of hours. Another method of reduction, which Is useful when the contrasts are already too severe, is the use of ammonium persulphate, as this attacks the highlights before the shadows. For using this mak° up a solution of the persulphate, 10 to 20 grains to the ounce of water. When reduced sufficiently. the action is quickly stopped by placing the negative in a 5 per cent. solution of sodium sulphite. Another error is under exposure. This gives a negative with very severe contrasts and great lack of detail in the shadows. For this error there is no remedy, but an improvement can often be effected by utilising some of the special brands of gaslight papers that are made for producing soft effects from strong' negatives. This will not get over the trouble of want' of detail in the shadows, but if the negative is valuable and it is not possible to take another, it is 119 well to try and cover up the shadows with a little pencil work on the negative. Pencil by itself will not readily adhere to the film, but the film should first be rubbed with what is known as a retouchor'e medium. This can be bought already prepared, or it may be made by dissolving resin in terpentine.

Daresbury.

DARESBURy INFANTS' SCHOOL.—A meeling of the ratepayers in tiro townships of Daresbnry, Newton-by- Daresbury and lieckwick 'was held in the Daresbury Parish Room on Monday eveeing, to arrange for an appeal to be made against the proposed closing of the above infant school. There was an attendance of about 50. The Rev.T.T. Lee-Jones occupied the chair and said he thought it would be advisable if he gave them a history of the case up to the present moment. There was, he said, a meeting originally held in the Daresbury Council Schools between the members of the Administrative Committee and the managers of the schools. Among other questions brought up Tr as that of removing the infants in standard one at Daresbury school to Moore Council School and Preston Brook Council School. Nothing was definitely decided then. After a short time the managers of the Daresbury Schools were called to meet the Administrative Sub. Committee at Crewe. loth Mr. Billington and himself spoke on behalf of the inmates. So far as he could remember the matter was referred to the sub-committee of Dutton. When the committee met again at Dutton the question was again shelved in that way, and a committee was appointed to meet the managers of Daresbury School again. A- meeting was held in the Daresbury Sessions Bobm; but unfortunately, he (the Vicar) had to leave before the meeting was over. Immediately he loft a resolution as brought forward which proposed that- the infants should be sent away from Daresbury School. (Gies of "Shame.") That. was carried by the chairmaa's vote only. If he (the Vicar) had been present be thought his vote would have • turned the matter on the other side, and they would have carried their point. That having taken place the matter was brought up again at Dutton, and the resolution was carried. He understood that certain schemes were brought forward with regird to the enlargement of Daresbu-y school. One was that the architect should prepare plans for the enlargement of the school if the infants were retained, and another scheme was for the building to be repaired if the infants were banished. He (the speaker) had moved and spoken at every meeting possible to defend the infants—(hear, bear)—but on every occasion they had been in the minority. Mr. Spruce said if the infants were excluded it would mean that they would have to go to Hatton, Moore, or

Mr. C. W. Phillips said he was a manager of the eb on], and it bad hem said that he was against the infants being retained at Daresbury. That was not so. Tbe present accommodation was ample, and he thought they ought to be allowed to go on as they were. He would support any schemo that would allow the infants to remain there, providing they had not to have the school enlarged. The Chairman said he thought it would be a good thing if the school managers would join unanimously and suggest to the County Committee that they, as managers of the Daresbury School, felt that for all those sears the school had been good enough to hold 200 scholars, and now ttn-re wet.° only 171 on the books, and make a stirring appeal to the committee to leave the school as it was, and leave the infants with it. Mr. Spruce suggested that the Vicar should be appointed to attend the sub-committee meeting at Dutton, to make an appeal' for the school to go on as at present. On the proposition of Mr. Shepherd Cross, seconded by Mr. James Tinsley, this was decided upon. On the suggestion of Mr. Shepherd Cross, it was decided that all present sign their names to the appeal.

===== 0002601_19090424_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
Padgate.

WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE.-A LIVELY MEETING.

Padgate bad its first women's suffrage meeting on Monday evening and many of the sportive young gentlemen of the village appeared to regard it as an opportunity for boisterous amusement too good to be missed. -The Church schoolroom was filled to over- flowing and the noisy element soon made itself manifest the Chairmen's speech being greeted with a running fire of interruptions.- At one time the disturbers became so troublesome that the meeting was at a stand- still for five minutes, hut peace was restored rald the speakers were allowed to plead their cause with com- paratively little •hindrance. Mr. E. Osborne presided and was supported by Miss Roden (Liverpool), Messrs. W. Mason and E. England. The Chairman explained that the meeting was one of a series Promoted by the Warrington Women's Suffrage Society and was the first of its kind that had been hold at Padete. Mr. Osborne was proceeding to advocate the claims of women who paid taxes to equal tepresentation with men, when ,the young men who had assembled in a body at the beck of the room began to laugh heartily. Mr. Osborne said he hoped the boys at the lack of the room would show some respect to those who had come to listen to the speeches. Evidently the description 'boss" was resented, for a distnrbance ensued and it was almost five minutes before order was re-tored. Continuing his speech Mr. Osborne said women wanted nothing which was not granted to men. Their claim was fair, equitable and just. The interruptions were renewed, cud for some time the meeting was in a state approaching pandemonium. Miss Boyden roured oil on the troubled waters by telling the disturbers that the great majority of those present bad come to listen to her and not to them. A great many bad noises did not make one good argument —(hear, hear)—but if any at the back ot the room had any arguments to make, she would allow them to come to the platform and make their speech at once. She would undertaie to arswer their arguments, but she could not compete against their noise. The Chairman said he was not going to stem! there and attempt to speak while slot of bo,s were whistling, cooing and behaving like nincompoops. He was surprised that in a place like Padgate, with the education the boys had had, they should act so nonsensically. If they had anything to sly let them wait until the proper time and say it. Opportunity would be afforded for questions to be put. He asked them to have some little 'respect for the name of Padgate. That. day was Primrose Day and it would be appropriate if he read them an extract from a letter by the late Lerd Beaconsfield with regard to Women's suffrage. He (the Chairman) was conscientiously of opinion that if Lord Beaconsfield were in power to-day the question of women's suffrage would be sot tied and women granted the vote. Referring to the fact that women who we-e considered capable of voting on municipal questions were debarred from exercising the Parliamer.tary franchise, Lord Beaconsfield wrote : " As I believe this anomaly to be injurieus to the best int rests of the country, I trust to see it removed by the Wiedom of Parliament." Mr. Osborne concluded by reading a letter from Alderman Bennett, Ruling Councillor of the Padgate Habitation of the Primrose League, in which he stated : " Whilst I strongly deprecate many of the tactics of the more militant suffragists. lam a profound believer in the principle of votes for womrn and do not doubt that it is destined to achieve eventually success. I trust my native village will to.dalght declare itself upon the side of freelom and of progress." - - - -

. - Some of the youths who had been distinguishing themselves by interruptions now; began to operate with noxious chemicals. A smart scuffle ensued and the impression conveyed was that someone was, being forcibly ejected. The Rev. G. W. Dickinson, vicar of Padgate, was also heard indignantly rebuking the disturbers and calling upon them to behave like men and not like I..ads. One of the village policemen made a timely appearance and the meeting calmed down. Miss Boyden, after an appeal to the chivalry of men, said there was one point on which all—seffregists and anti-suffragists alike—could agree, and that was that woman's most sacred work and her greatest happiness would in the vast majority of cases be found in her own home. On that point nobody felt more strongly than she did. If there was a'ny danger of women's suffrage distracting women from that work, there was at least one suffragette who ,would resign her membership of all suffrage societies. (Applause.) While, however, they were agreed on that poeit, they were divided about what would be _the best way to do the work. They, as members of the Women's Suffrage Society, thought that women to do their work efficiently should have the vote. There were usually three arguments Used against women's suffrage. First it was said women wore not physically strong enough, secondlyethey were not intelligent enough, and thirdly that they were not good enough to have the vote. It was perfectly true that they were not as strong as men and they could not be soldiers, Lut she would point out that there were practically- no soldiers in the British army who had votes. Men were not disquali- fied because they were lame, or blind, or because they were Quakers whose principles forbade them to fight. The vote did not depend upon physical strength in this Country, but upon property. Moreover. i the vote was not based n this country on intelligence. there were many future legislators in that room that

night, and she did not know that they had shown them- selves particularly intelligent. (Loud laughter). The vote was not given to the women who had carried off the examinations at the University, and it was not denied to the 8,000 men at the last general election who.could neither read nor write. If the vote depended upon a competitive examination in English history she thought a gre 4 many women would beat the men. (Laughter). No, the vote in England was given simply and solely on property and she was there to support the demand that it. should be given to women on the same terms was it is or might be given to men. The great principle of no taxation without representation was only carried out where men were concerned. Women had to pay taxes, although they were denied the vote. If Mr. Asquith would not attempt to pass a law recognizing the soond principle that taxation and representation went together, be ought to introduce another Bill removing all taxation from women.( Laughter and applause). If they could not have the vote, they should not pay. It had also been said Abet women bad no use for the vote, as they could always get what they wanted without it. Having protested against the "monstrous mis- conception" that there was any conceivable subject on which all the women would be found on one side and all the men on the other, Miss Boyden contended that womanly influence as at present exercised in politics was not a wholesome influence. It meant that men voted a certain way not because they were cell- vinced, but because the lady cenvaaser put on her best bat and prettiest frock and tried what Mr. Henry Arthur Jones called " sweedling." There were many members in the House of Commons who were there largely by the assistance of women, and by not pushing forward the movement in favour of women's suffrage they were guilty of dishonourable conduct. If Women bad any business in politics at all, they had the right to vote. (Applause.) It was because woman was largely interested in the home that she ought to have the vote. Woman was in the home all day while the man went out. (A Voice: "And Isings home the cash" and laughter.) Politics touched women at every point, and it would, she conteneed, give them an increased. sense of responsihilitv if they were able to exercise the franchise. It -bad been stid that; the grant of the vote to women would place the govern- ment of this country under the control of women, but she would point out that by the Bill at present advocated by the suffrage societies, there would only be from one and a half to two millions of women voters against seven and a half millions of men. In conclusion Miss Royden said we were wasting a great asset of progress when we refused to the en otional women who could bring to the State such a driving power, the steadying influence of responsibility, the right to t .ke a direct part in7the government of the 'country: She moved a resolution pledging the meeting to support the granting of the vote to women on the same terms as it is or may be granted to men. Mr. W. Mason, in seconding, said Lancashire " bad always been known for its sportsmanlike character, hut at the beginning of that meeting Lancashire did not show itself in its true colours. (A Voice: "No sarcastic remarks, please.") He would like to remind that voluble gentlemen that there had recently been passed an Act which dealt with the upsetting of public meetings— an Act which WAS specially passed against women upsetting the meetings of men—and he wondered how they would feel if the Chairman rsquested the representative of the law to put that Act into force •and let the voluble gentlemen feel the full effect of it. (Laughter.) This was a slow-moving country. Nothing short of a political earthquake or political expediency over led our Government to see the urgency for a great 1 measure • hence, in season and out of season they might expect the women to urge this matter until the very deafest of Cabinet Ministers would listen to the.,knock- ing without their gates. Women in every aalk of life were competing successfully with men. Mentally they were man's equal and even from the point of view of physical endurance they were not greatly his inferior. The question of women's suffrege was no longer an academic question, but one of practical importance. (Applause). Miss Boyden then answered a batch of questions. One read :-,--" Do you advocate that womsn when they Tiive got the vote should represent their own sex in the House of Commons" ? Miss Boyden said that at present they er e asking for the vote and nothing but the vote, and she believed the majority of vt omen suffragists did not require or wish for anything more. Personally, it seemed to her that. it any constitu,ncy chose to elect a woman, there was no possible reason why ,a woman should not represent it. On a vote being taken the reselution was carried, but a large proportion of the audience refrained from voting. Mr. E. England, in moving a vote of thanks to Miss Boyden, said he would not consider himself worthy of the name of man if he did net support the cause of Women's Suffrage. Miss Wilkinson in seconding said they heard a great deal about "Britons never shall be slavels" but it appeared that when some of the boys present grew up to be men they would make their mothers and sisters into slaves. _

..... The vote was heartily pissed and the meeting con- cluded.

CONSERVATIVE DEMONSTRATION. Sir Joseph Lawrence on Tariff Reform. The platform of the Lymm Drill Hall makes a picturesque setting for public meetings. The speakers discourse amidst charming woodland scenery and, what- everone's views as to the subject matter and the method of its exposition, the artistic effect is altogkher pleas- ing to the eye. On Wednesday evening the Conserva- tives of Lymm held a demonstration in the hall, Sir Joseph Lawrence, one of the leading advocates of tariff reform, being ,tha princ.ipet speaker. • At the advettised time for the opening of the proceedings there was only a sparse attendance, but as the evening wore on the audience attained respectable dimensions. Enthusiasm did not run high and bad it not been for the rlemonstratioes of an individual on the front row who added" hear, hear " to the most obvious coremon places and went into paroxysms of cheering at frequent intervals, the proceedings would hsve fallen somewhat flat. Mr. W. Peter Rylands presided and was supported on tile platform by Sir Joseeh Lawrence,Dr. T. Eastham (prospective Unionist candidate for Hyde), Mr. 11. J. Spencer, Mr. H. Carey (secretary of the County Unionist Club), Hon. j. E. Cross, Messrs. J. Palmer, H. B. Boumphrey, %V. Callender, A. Ewart, W. P. Al acken- drick, J. Arnold and T. Ridgway. Letters of apology for non-attendance had been received from Mr. John Coxon, Mr. W. 11. Bleckly, Mr.- Moseley (Agden), and Mr. Newton. Mr. Bleckly wrote :—" I think it is the duty of all Censervatives to do their utmost to assist in getting rid of this very worst Government of modern times as quickly as possible. It is gratifying to find that a large pro- portion of thinking and patriotic Liberals are coming round to this opinion. I myself am honoured with the acquaintance of a number of such men who at the next general election will swell the ranks of the Con- servatives and so deserve the gratitude cf their country." The Chairman observed that to attack the present Government was almost like bitting a man when he was down. The by-electioes during the past two years were an extraordinarily striking mani- festation of the detestation in which the Govern- ment was held by the people of this country. At the last general election the Government gained the support not only of the ordinary Radical vote but. of every section of extremists, whether they were extreme temperance people, or the extreme Noncon- formist element, or any extreme party who thought they would try and get something out of the now Government— The result had been that each section had been disgusted with the Government bemuse it had not done suaicient for them or they were dissatisfied with what it had done for others, but the ones who had been most disgusted were those moderate Liberals who really were Liberals and %the wished to see the old Liberal principles put into operation. He did not believe the present Government _could claim to have any tine principles. Their policy was a purely adventitious ene—that of trimming their sails to catch the most favourable breeze. He was confident they only required a general election to return once more to Parliament a Conservat lye Government whose policy WAS the development of the trade of the country, the maintenance of the Empire and social reform. (Applause). • Sir Josepn Lawrence: a robust-looking gentlemen, with a rather thin voice and a very fluent style of speech, dealt solely with Tariff Reform-ea subject which he said was "very near and eesr to him." His particular interest in the question was owing to the strong feeling which he had ale as bad of admiration for Mr. Chamberlain and the policy which he pro- poueded some five years ago. net policy Was forced upon him not only by various recommendations of conferences of the Colonies but by - the th wets which were held out by Germany to us in the year 1903, because of the Prefereace that Germany gave to this country in its commercial )(silt ions. It became necessary fot Mr. Chamberlain to come out into the open and propound definitely the policy of closer relations with our kinsmen acrest the seas. That policy rallied to his support great bumbers of men both in Parliament and outside it, and though Mr. Chan-,ber- hie was momentarily laid low by sickness it must be gratirying to him and to all frieede and well-wishers of the ca use to know that the cause was steadily pro- gressing and not only Progressing tut growing by leaps bounds throbghout the Empire. "I say frankiy continued Sir Joseph "that I have ;s great and traditional reverence for the name of Mr. Cobden. I was one of that generation, though a youdg men, who followed Mr. Cobden in the days when the reepectol father of your chairman was the Radical member 'of Parliament for Warrington. I was a Radical, myse'f and a slang Free Trader, an I to-day I hold the rant° belief in the principle and doctrine of free exchange if it were attainable—which' it is not. You can find in the writings of Cobden himself arguments and justi.fleaticems for reviewing your domeetic policy ent if need I e; revising it and adapting it to the conditions of the day in which 30u find yourselves. Cobden himself was no slavish admirer of precedent. If it interfered with the comfort or happiness of the people be brushed it aside. To-day we are face to face with two bed rock facts—the necessity for markets for our manufactures and the necessity for more opportunities for employment for our people."( Applause). Sir Joseph asked his hearers to guerd against the policy of looking at 130 trd of Trade returns of what our exports and imports were to-day as compared with fifty years ago. What they had to look at was ; our relative position will other ceuntrics. Germany might be cited as a signal ,example, for according to a Board of Trade return, taking two periods of five years each and comparing them, it was found that in those periods the export trade of Germany in manufactured goods had grown to the extent of 62 millions sterling per annum, whereas ours had only grown to the extent of two millions. The system of Free Trade had broken down. "I don't say Free Trade," said Sir Joseph, "but Free Imports, because Free Traits we heve never had." (Applause). Free Tiede was not an article of religious faith. It was a mete question of business expediency. Some people adhered to it with an obstinacy which was worthy of its being a fetish, but it was, after all, a mere question of commercial opportunism and expedi- ency, and if we could diimiss the historical and tradi- tional consideratiobs and view it purely as a matter of commonsense bueiness, theme was not a man of common sense who would not admit that the case form, recon- sideration and revision and if necessary an alterat ion of our existing policy had been completely established.

With regard to the iron and steel industry, there was a time when England was able to turn out 6,000,000 tons of pig iron, Germany 2,000,000 odd tons and America the same quantity. What was the position to-day? After thirty years America was turn'ng out 25,000,000 tons a year and Germany 12,000,000 tons, and Euglaud I only 10,000,000 tons. As to the cotton trade Lancashire used to clothe 800,000,000 of people in the East,but we had been manufectming and shipping out to the East machinery which would obviously be used by the people there in manufacturing the same textile fabrics that we manufactured in Lsncashire. Our great aseendancy in that trade was beginning to be assailed, and was bound to be assailed with ieereasing intensity as years went on, because there were untold millions of men in the East who were able to lise much more cheaply and work longer hours than the workers in Lancashire. Did they think the Lancashire cotton trade was the one trade of all trades that was going to be free from competition? Competition, if it bad not begun, would come upon us just as surely as the sun rose in tbe heavens every day. In view of that possibility we ought to be thinking out what steps we ought to take to guard ourselves and protect—and he used the word in its hest sense, although he was not a Protectionist , himself—to protect ourselves in a legitimate sense against the time when we would to beset with the com- petition that was inevitable. Dealing eith the question of the export of capital, Sir Joseph said capital had ' always gone out of this country in the sense of being invested abroad, but it had been surplus capital which could be spared from our accumulated resources and profits. In the last three years, however, the tate at which capital had been put into foreign investments amounted to an increase of over £400,000,000. (A voice: "We have got it.") "You have got it" said Sir Joseph "in the sense that monies you have invested in British ind ustries you are selling out and with the proceeds investing in foreign investments. (A voile : "We get the interest back.") Simultaneously with the invest- ment in foreign concerns you have had a depreciath n mainly in British securities of £249,000,600, that is to say that stocks and shares in English concerns have declined by £249,000,000 and you have invested £400,000,000 in foreign concerns. Why? Lord Rothes- child said because of the fears of legislation and taxa- tion which were likely to be carried out by the present Government. On the other hand there are those who say it is because your home investments are not made attractive, your dividends and profits are diminishing while the profits and dividends of foleign concerns am o increasing." Our present tariff system was unscientific. We taxed things we could not make ontselves and let in bee the things that, we could make. Sir Joseph contended that the-ques- tions of corruption and public purity were entirely separate and distinct frem the question of tariffs. Ile admitted there was eorruption in America but it was found mainly among the municipalities. The only power which had to deal with the tariff in America was the Federal Government sitting in Washington, and there it was -impossible for anybody to point to any specific instance of corruption. In conclusion Sir Joseph said they wanted to implant, in the minds of their countrymen the necessity of some change which would tend not only to conserve the manufacturing and produethe industries of this country in order to give employment but in order to produce the revenue for the necessary expenses of the State. It was a commonplace in Lopdon that this was the last Free Trade Budget that was ever likely to be iutroduced in this country. The Government had reached the point when they were at their wits' end to find the revenue necessary to conduct the business of the State. The question of the navy was being held up because they had not the ready money to pay for the Dreadnoughts. (Apples: ). Dr. 1. stham, a comparatively. young politician who speaks • ith confidence and delibera tion,said the gentle- man is I, interrupted seemed to be proud that we had got money o send abroad. (A Voice: "He is a Liberal '). "1 ea. g not any doubt as to the gentleman's political faith " said the elector "1 should think he is ODO of the blind followers of Mr. Asquith." (The interrupter referred to: "Allow me to toll you I can think for myself. I don't wish you to do it for me "). Dr. Eastham contended that much of the capital which was being spent abroad should to kept here to energize our own industries. He proceeded to give the follow- ing picturesque description of the achievements of the Government as they appeared to him :—" It has locked up 72:3 suffragettes. It has daily denounced the Lords,and yet it. has made 28 new Peers. lt bas appointed a Ito3al Commission to inquire what is whisky, as if some of our teetotal advocstes did not know what whisky was. (Here the enthusiast in the front row cheered violently). It has passed a measure that will enable us to get small-pox much cheaper and easier. It has issued a proclamation that in future the trousers in the Navy should only be cne inch wider at the foot than at the knee. It has passed an Act or Parliament to enable fat policemen to chase little boys and rob them of their Woodbines. It reenacted the Chinese slavery ordinance and last but not least, it passed a great measure of social reform—an 'Act of Parliament that enables a man to marry twice and only get one mother-in-law." Con- tinuing, the Doctor accused " this deceitful dangerous Government" of trifling with the unemployed question and said the Licensing Bill was quite unnecessary, because everybody knew that the nation was becoming more sober every day under Mr. Balfour's Act. Mr. Carey, who in his enthusiasm contrived to upset a glass of water, moved a resolution condemning the Ipolicy of the government particularly in regard to the defensive forces of the Crown, and expressing the belief that only by the policy of constiuctivo teiritf reform can the necessary revenue be obtained for the mainte- nance of our supremacy on the ee as. Mr. Spencer,.a budding orator of sixteen, seconded in a speech which Sir Joseph Lawrence said was charactesised by great breadth of judgment and states- manship. '1 he resolut ion was carried but not unanimously. On the motion of the lion. J. E. Cross seconded by Mr. Ewart the speakers were thanked, and a similar complin.ent to the chairman concluded the meeting.

A special meeting of the Lyrnm Urban District Council was held on Monday night in the Town Hall for the purpose of electing officers and for the trans• action of other business. There were present :—Messrs- Moston, 0. G. Smith, Dewhurst, Groves, Welfcrd4 Gotobed, Warburton. Davies Ewart, Murnford, Donald- son (inspector), and W. Milliard (clerk). Mr. Mullard, mho was voted to the chair pro tern announced the result of the recent elections.

Mr. Groves, in proposing Mr. 0. G. Smith to the position of Chairman of the Council for the next twelve months, said that he had thoroughly earned the honour and he had no doubt that he would fill the position with credit.

Mr. Davies seconded and it was carried unanimously

Mr. 0. G. Smith then took the chair, and in returnin'i thanks for the honour they bad done him said he felt that it was not due to any merit of his own but merely l.y f._lux of time that he succeeded to the office.

Mr. Moston (the ex-chairman) in offi ring a word of welcome to the new Chairman said that although he had not any badge of office to band down he had a key which Eerved as an insignia. He then banded the Chairman the key of tie seals of the Council. Continuing, Mr. Aloston proposed Mr. T. H. Was burton as vice-chairman.

This having been seconded, Mr. Davies proposed the name of Mr. G. P. Dewburst as vice-chairman. At this point Mr. Dowhurst rose and said that although he was quite in touch with the work of the Council he was so much away from home that he could not give the number of attendances desirable. There were no other nominations, so Mr. Warburton was unanimously elected. Mr. Warburton replying, said he was sure that the interest be took in the work of that Council would not deteriorate in any shape or form, whilst he was in that office. He was very pleased to tee that they had a new memt er of the Council in Mr. Gotobed and he Loped he would put in as much work as they did. He (the speaker) would do his best to help Mr. Smith and the rest of the Council during the next 12 months. The Chairman proposed a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. Moston for his success in the chair during the last two years. No man could be more assiduous, more attentive or more diligent tban he and he bad earned the gratitude of the Council and of the public whom he had PO well served.

Mr. Warburton seconded and it was supported by Mr. Groves. The motion was elrried. _ _

Mr. Moston in responding said he was not so expect- ant of so hearty a vote of thanks to him. He bad been amply repaid in many ways. The work bad Eepn pleasure to him night after night. 3he meetings he had attended had been to his physical and mental advantage. It was resolved tlat the whole of the members of the Council constitute the various committees. Mr. Dewhurst was elected chairman and Mr. Davies vice-chairman of the Roads Committee; the Rev. G. B. Thurston, chairman of the Sewage Farm Committee: Mr. Welford, chairman of the Fire Engine and Building Committee; Mr. Groves, chairman of the Gas Com- mittee; Mr. Mosten, chairman of the Finf.nce Com- mittee; and Mr. Smith, chairman, and Mr. Warburton, vice-chairman of the General Purposes Committee. Bd. made by Mr. F. NI. Adams, district auditor, at his audit of the accounts for the year ended the 31st March, 1908 of the Urban Distritt Council of Lymm. Mr. J. Howes and £5.

Sir,—Your correspondent J. Royle is evidently still emarting'under the effect of the defeat 'of his party Vustalued in East 'Denbigh. I have no knowledge of the gentleman except that I came across him at an open-air meeting during the Welsh contest. His version of the £5 incident is a complete fable although I don't suppose that will intrrest your readers much. The only point is his desire to debate. Concerning this matter I must refer him to the Warrington Tariff 3." Reform League. If they care to put him forward cs • their representative and they are willing to bear half the expenses of the meeting, I will name a local Association willing to act on my behalf.—Yours truly, J. HOWES.

Warrington, April 20th, 1007.

The " Daylight Saving" Bill. I see from your issue of the 17th inst. that your anonymous correspondent with astronomical tenden- cies again returns to the attick, although in a lees virulent manner than previously. Instead of condemning the Bill as " monstrously absurd and. impracticable" we now find a more moderate opposition, based on the dread (1) that the workpeoyle of this country will bave to go to work in the dark during its operation and (2) that employers will have to spend a correspondingly increased sum on artificial illuminants. Passing over the fact that one does not necessarily follow from the other, as it Might be dark on getting up and still light enough to work when one got there, it does seem a pity that such statements should be lightly issued, as they may prejudice people against a proposed measure which would he of the greatest benefit to the community generally, and a reasonable Examination of the Bill would have prevented your correspondent from making them ; but as I have no reason to doubt his sincer:ty allow me to assure him that there is no likelihood, if the Act passes, of his 'fears being realised; Anyone who will take the trouble to plot a curve of the times of sunrise and sunset between mid-April and mid-September can satisfy himself that rising at 5.20 in the morning will not necessitate getting up in the dark, owing to the fact, as every bicyclist is aware, that it is licht for one hour before sunrise. As regards the artificial light, so far from any more being required there would under this head be an enormous saving. It has been calculated that the cost of al tificial light for each unit of the population averages one-tenth of a penny per head per hour. This figure has I believe, been generally accepted, and as under the proposed Bill the number of hours during which the the cost of artificial light would be s3aved would be approximately one hundred and fifty per annum, this would mean a minimum nett annual saving of two million sterling, so that I repeat that four-fiftba of the interest on one hundred million pounds sterling of the National Debt could be saved by the people of Great Britain and Ireland at the cost of moving the hands of the clock once in the Spring and once again in the Autumn. A perusal of the objections raised before the second Select Committee of the House of Commons' which is now sitting, and the answers to these, both of which will shortly be available to the public, will amply repay anyone who is interested in this measure, and incidentally will dispose of a good niany fallacies respecting it.—Yours faithfully, HOLMAN' KINGDON. "Bill Crest," Frodsham, Cheshire. 19th April, 1909.

Temperance and Employment.

Sir,—l- litTve been following the -correspondence of Mr. Parker and L.O.V. with great interest, and would like to say a few words on the question. Although I am not a Socialist, I agree with L.O.V. as to the nationalization of some industries but not all. For instance, Mr. Parker must acknowledge that if the Drink Trade were under State control such cases as "Tom Jones" would become rare. Also I

heartily agree with the ultimate aim of Sos.ialism, to promote the brotherhood of man, but Socialists overlook the fact, filet a scheme such as theirs would only be practicable when the brotherhood was an accomplished fact. I dislike also the attitude of Socialists to schemes for sccial reform. If the system does not agree with theirs in every detail, they criticise it most unmercifully, bet if anyone takes the liberty of criticising their scheme they are up in arms at once. I am speaking from experience, and L. 0. V.'s letter on the question is an example, since he must agree with Mr. Parker as to the harm the liquor trade dces to men's capabilities. So long as it is. to the interest of private persons to sell drink, the eaucation and re-generation of the working-classes will be diffi- cult in the extreme. "Tom Jones "under the influ- *nee of drink is a poor Social Reformer, but let him think, not drink, and he will soon seethe evil it brings and also the other evilirof capital oppressing labour. "What is wanted is a sfeme to limit the 'employer's power to oppress his workmen for purposes-of private gain, without- abolishing the relations altogether.— Yours for Social Reform, Penketh.

H. S.

Sir,—The fact that Ur. Parker has not contested the statements, or the reliability of the figures in my last letter, is, I take it, as good as an admittance on his part that the working consumer has not received the benefits obtained from efficiency in production. But he says that if Tom Jones will only 'spend the money usually spent in beer in other things, there would be such a change that trading would be increased 20 per cent., tradesmen and manufactories working ovettime &c. I have not the slightest hesi- tation in saying that this is about the biggest blunder he has made during the s whola etf this discussion. According to Mr. Parker £3,500 is spent weekly in alcholic liquors, and before proceeding further it would be as well to point out that this sum is not spent wholly by Tom Jones and his class. To divide this sum equally amongst the inhabitants of Warring- ton and say that five cr six shillings is spent per week per family is not a fair way to get the average spent in alcoholic drink. When ding so, we must not: forget that Fait of the population indulge in the expensive type of liquors. Some of our" well to- do" inhabitants will spend as much in buying one wine-glass full of liquor as will make Tom Jones drunk. Taking this fact into consideration, it is doubtful if the average of Tom Jones's class reaches more than two or three shillings per family. This is an important fact which our temperance friends should i bear n mind. Reverting to the point previously mentioned, Mr. Parker sketches a pen picture of the increase of trade accruingfrom the £3,500 now :spent in drink being spent Lin food, clothing, etc. This statement rests upon the assumption that the inhabi- tants of Warrington's spending power has been increased by .£3,500 weekly, which quite evidently is not the case. This amount has only beeniased in purchasing a 'different commodity, and the only altera- tion that would take place would be the men pre- viously engaged in the brewing, and relative trades would be engaged in expending their labour power in mannfacturing some other commodity which might be more beneficial to the community, and unemploy- ment would not be relieved one iota. -

Just let me illustrate. Labor Omma Vincit is a labourer receiving 24 shillings per week. He sees the folly of Xorn Jones in spending so much money in alcoholic drink and decides to become a total abstainer. L.O.V. dces not go to football matches or smoke like Tom Jones does and spends the money which Tcm Jones spends in these things, on some- thing more beneficial to his well-being. The difference being, Tom Jones contributes to the upkeep of breweries, etc., at d football dubs. L.O.V. con- tributes, to the employment of men making other commodities used by him. If Tom Jones stopped away from football matches and became a total abstainer he would disengage people employed in these businesses and, if he spent the money in food, clothing, etc. would engage people in the manu- facturing of those useful things, and no increase in employment would take place. The whole question is reduced to whether Tom spends his money wisely or not: I do not think he does. But we have it upon the authorities I quoted last week that if at least CO per cent of our population spot every penny they received in a wise manner the

poverty in our midst would still be appalling. And I should just like to say here in passing that Tom Jones's case is exceptional. Fcr every similar case Mr. Parker can show me I will show him 20 or more the extreme reverse. I know a young man,-a lifelong teetotaller, who has had three homes sold through unemployment, and curiously enOugh the same man will shout three cheers for a "great and glorious " Empire every time the "Daily Mail" asks him to. The working man may spend more in a concotion called the national beverage than his slender income allows. But let Mr. Parker live amongst this class and notice the frugality, thrift, and managing capabilities, the same as I have done and he will marvel. The temperance question from a moral and physical point of view I deem to be important-. Its bearing upon the economic conditions 1 believe is greatly exagger- ated. But whether the producer chooses to spend his product wisely or not should not prevent him from receiving the whole of what he produces. Mr. George Barnes, M.P. aptly put it uPon one occasion in the House of Commons. He said that" he thought Tom Jones had more right to spend his product in beer and tobacco than someone else had to spend it in win'e and cigars." I do not want Mr. Parker to misunderstand me. As I have already intimated I am a teetotaler, non-smoker and a vegetarian. I wish Mr. Parker and everyone else were. But Ido not say if these became univet sql customs the millennium wculd be then attained. We have /thousands of women workers in England who do not spend a penny on drink, or in watching football matches. The Irish consume about one-half as much as the English people and their ccndition deplorable. The Indian ryot, the Qb.inese coolie do not consume alcohol either, and are about the poorest people on the globe. Economic oppression is the great evil and monopoly of land and capital are at the root of it. lam not a leader in the party to which I belong. I have burnt the "midnight oil" in fathoming and trying to under- starrd our complex sccial problems and am d, ing my best, however little that may be, to bring about a better state of things ; correspondenco in the press being one of the spheres in which I work. The sickly sordidness and low idealism pervading every sphere of commercial activity, bolds the in horror. The system which has brought me down to the level of a machine I hate venomously. And although I have not seen the beauty of a 26th summer, I pledge the remaining tenure allotted to me upon this earth in trying to persuade my co-inhabitants to use it in a more reasonable manner, and to that end will the enctavour be directed of yours sincerely, LABOR OMNIA. VINCIT.

Sir John Brunner and Free Trade. Spirited Denial of Tory Charges.

Hotel Bellevue, Cannes, 17th April, 1909. Sir,— On the 26th of last month, the "Runcorn Weekly News " published a report of what it described as a " very successful meeting under the auspices of the Northwich DiVision Conservative and Unionist Association." It is sometimes one thing to attain success, and quite another to deserve it. Part of the success in this case was due to a speech made by Mr. John Henry Cooke, the well-known solicitor of Winsford, who when I first made his acquaintance was, like myself, a Nonconformist and a Liberal. I remember well the amusement it gave me in 1885, to point out that every one of the big-wigs who supported my opponent, was like him, the son of a father who had been a Nonconformist and a Liberal, that they had all been successful in life, and had all become "respectable." During the course of the speech by which Mr. Cooke contribut«l to the success of the meeting, he said "Sir John Brunner, their Member, posed on the platform as the greatest Free Trader that ever was, but when he took his seat in the office of Messrs. Brunner Mond and Co. he was the greatest Pro- tectionist that ever was." That be used these words is certain, but you will see the gloss he puts upon them in the subjoined copy of his letter to me, the result of my ordinary polite enquiry whether the report failly represented his speech. , Of course he said "your" Member not "their" Member, and so on. That is thoroughly understood. Mr. Cooke, to make the meeting "very successful," publicly brands as a hypocrite a man whom in private life he affects to treat as a friend. What is a Free Trader? The answer is, one who holds a certain view cf the wisdom of a certain line of national policy. I am a Free Trader, convinced and inveterate. It is obviously true that if the voters of the United Kingdom hereafter decide to put duties on the imports of Alkalies, the United Alkali Co., and Brunner,Mond and Co., will be able to raise their prices for Alkalies, but I say that the voters will be very unwise if they come to that decision. My view is that the nation as a whole is best served when everybody is left free to buy and to sell as he or she pleases, no matter what duties other nations gather in at their custom houses. You will see in Mr. Cooke's letter to me a long story about my Company's dealings with the United States, a matter as to which he is naturally all abroad. _

It has. been the habit of my political opponents for nearly a quarter of a century to attack my methods in trade, and they have always made a mess of it. He slys that with a view of meetipg the McKinley Tariff we took an interest in an American Works. _

Now I don't think it will hurt anybody if I tell you the facts. The staring of the Wirks in question had nothing on earth to do with the McKinley Tariff or any other Tariff. It occurred many years before that Tariff was heard of, and they were started in opposition to us. At a later date, but still several years before Mr. McKinley came on the scene, the promoters thought fit to invite our co-operation. What now becomes of all Mr. Cooke's diatribe about the 8,000 men we might now be employing? It may perhaps occur to you that Mr. Cooke might have learnt the truth of the matter by asking me for it.

That is however not so. If be had asked me any such question, I Should have told him, in polite phraseology to go to Jericho Yours faithfully,

JOHN BRUNNER.

[corY]. Crossfield House, . Winsford, Cheshire, March 30th, 1909.

Dear Sir John,—Your lettei of the 27th was only posted in London on Sunday the 28th and received by me yesterday, the 29th inst. I had nct seen the "Runcorn Weekly News" or indeed any newspaper report of the meeting held at Runcorn last Wednes- day. I therefore wrote for copies which only an ived by this afternoon's post. The report of my speech its necessarily condensed. I was not aware that any- thing I said would be sufficiently worthy of your notice or admiration. I spoke without notes but my remembrance is quite clear as to what I said. I stated, as I believe to be the fact (1) that prior to the McKinley Tariff, Brunner, Mond and Co. were sending to the United States a large proportion of their products and that after that Tai if became operative your 'firm's trade with the States was very considerably reduced owing to the operati nof the Tariff. (2) That with a view of meeting the effect of the Tariff and the consequential loss to English trade, your Company, I understood, took an interest in wmks or secured additional agencies there, so as to bring back to the Company but not to the nation, the Trade which had been lost owing to the Tariff. (3) That, if the Tariff had not been introduced the works at Wilmington would probably have soon doubled the number of hands employed and that I understood you now employed about 4,000 hands; so that the effect of the Tariff was to reduce the number of hands which would have been employed if the Tariff had not been brought into operation. Thus without the Tariff there was a chance of 8,000 being employed at Winnington. (4) I said that I admited the business capacity and intelligence dis- played by your firm in the conduct of their business and that everybody knew it had been a most successful Company, arid that, if you, when you appeared on the political platform would pcse, not as a Free Trader, but as one whose company had been built up, with you in the chair, by the effect of a restrictive policy upon competitors, it would give greater satisfaction and be more beneficial to the nation at large. If trade restrictions were good for Brunner Mond, they were good for the naticn at large, and the voters of the Northwich Division might rest assured that the re4trictive policy proposed by Tariff Reformers, with reference to free imports, could not have the damag- ing effect which you advocated upon the political platform. The above is the sum and substance of what I stated. lam sure ycu will not dispute the point that Brunner Mond has been an extremely successful concern. _

Further everybody knows that English trade was

THE LECTURE.

In the evening a crowded audience assembled in the Church and listened with intense, interest and pleasure to Mr. Rattenbury when he lectured On "The relation of nonconformity to p)litics generally." Alderman Bennett presided over the meeting and was supported by the Rev. Thomas Gladding, Mi. John Bennett and Dr. Bennett. The Chairman referred to the recent death of an ex- Wesleyan President Dr. Rigg, and said be would be greatly missed by Methodists. He was a great Con- servative leader in his day. Still there were young men who were stepping into the gap in the great army and they were marching on. They had one who had stepped into of the army in the Rev. Mr. Rattenbury, whom he was pleased to see that night. Dr. Rigg represented the old idea, and Mr. Rattenoury the new. Mr. Ratten- bury was always a welcome visitor to Padgate. (Applause). The subject of his lecture was a serious thing. Nonconformity and politics appealed to him (the speaker) a good deal. His personal opinion was that the Church should be absolutely free from party politics. It was quite right for a Christian man to hold politics in a broader sense than be bound to a party. His experience of party politics was that they thought and voted as their leaders told them. He was not sure that party politics did not retard real proeress. We were a long way from being like brothers, and party politics rather tended to make us less brotherly. If our Christianity were paramount to our party politics,we must take an interest in politics.On all the great fundamental questions we agreed and we ought to do what was embodied in Christianity. Christianity, it seemed to him, was infinitely deeper than our sectarian divisions. The display of human love would obliterate parties, for human love was greater to us than any party tie. In concluding, he said : "Keep away from party politics." (Applause). The Lecturer, in his introductory remarks, said that the Chairman had spoken of Dr. Rigg as a leader of Conservatism. The speaker knew for a fact that at one time Dr. Rigg was called "That old Radical." (Laughter.) The subject before them that evening was a most lively one. It was particularly interesting to Methodism, but the Methodist Press had been very much agitafed by 4. Dr. Lidgett's recent denunciation of the House of Lords had created a good deal of criticism by one paper of the Methodist Church—he would not tell them what paper it was because he did , not want to advertise it. (Laughter.) It was always the same when a man stood out for what was right. The question was of wider interest than Methodism. The daily press in London had exer- cised itself very considerably about the supposed political propaganda of certain Free Church ministers—fs;-e.ially in Central London—and it had been said that the Free Churches were being run not in the interest.; of the Kingdom of God bat in the interests of the Liberals or of the Socialists. The various letters and articles which had occurred ire the daily press so far as they referred to the Wesleyan body, were nothing but a gross fabric of lies. He did not speak from the standpoint of a party politician. He belonged to no political party what- ever, and he would confess to them that he had voted for most political parties at one time or another. He had never yet voted for the Independent Labour Party but he dare say that the time would come when he would. A Free Church minister in a book bad accused Free Churches of to-day of having forsaken their old spiritual ideals and of using the Church for political ends instead of using political means for the ends of the Church. The Church had also been accused and criticised for not producing saints, but only politicians. They had been told in that same book that the spirituality of the Free Chnrcbes had con- siderably degenerated since they took up their political propaganda. They had also been informed that the Free Church Council was a branch of the Liberal party. The great purpose of the book was to proclaim that however much individuals had a right to take a part in the political warfare of the country the Church had no right ehatever to go into the political sphere. Those opinions were held by a great body of pious and devout people of this country. The general argu- ment ,of the writer was that the Churches had no right to go collectively into the political sphere. Nonconformity had something to do with politics in the time of Oliver Ccomwell. If it were true that the Church existed just for a party they would be open to the strongest condemnation possible. (Applause.) If they were to be political at all it was better to be political in the national sense. In the 17th century the Church had used the political instrument for political ends. They cut off the head of Charles 1., and it was one of the best things that happened in this country. If the Church of Christ were in earnest to-day it would do away with tyrannies just as it did then. "If we are to be free men to-day," he went on, "we must do away with the House of Lords. (Applause.) And the same spiritual forces at work in the 17th century—by doing away with the tyranny of the King who crushed the individual force of the country—these forces will do away with any- thing that crush 3s the liberty of the people.' If we were more spiritual than in the 17th century we should be more political. It was argued to-day that the Free Church Council identified itself with , Liberalism, fie assured them, as a matter of fact, of the great diffionity in the Free Church Council Execu- tive for moderate people like himself was to prevent them chastising the Liberal Government. It was not true to say that the Free Church was a muerte of the Liberal party. He did not deny that they did alot for the Liberals it the last General election. It was because of the issues the Liberals took up in regard to education, temperance, and the liberties of the people. that the Council honestly felt that it was about the best way to help to bring about the Kingdom of God. (Applause). The Free Chu, ch Council would use any party to - promote the Kingdom of God. (Applause). They were told that they were producing politicians and not saints. In the first place he sheuld like to know what a saint was. At one time a man • was looked upon as a saint if he was an -ascetic. The truth was that the word saint had an unsavoury meaning to-day. He took it that a saint was a good man, a holy man, who loved God and His people, and if it were said that the Free Churches were not producing saints of that sort, all he could say was that his evidence was in a different direction. There were men and women living to-day who were not worthy of humanity. They wanted saints who could stand the difficulties of life, who could be in the world and yet not of it. It was not true to say that the Free Churches were not producing as many good men and women as they did in the 17th century. Jesus Chriat once said to his disciples" Ye are the salt of the earth, ye are the light of the world." He sometimes thought that those were the most wonderful things Christ ever said, When he said that to those men he said it to them collectively. Christ knew that upon his disciples depended the salvation of men. A Christian ,Church must witness with its character and be the light of the world. The witness should be by spoken testimony. The Church did not exist just to make good men, but to take its conspicuous place in the world. (Applause.) The Church had no right to get in a corner. (Hear, hear). They had no right to hide behind all sorts of conventionalities and ecclesiastical conditions and to run away when there was a fight. (Applause.) The lecturer mentioned how Lord Shaftesbury was

greatly impeded when he introduced the Factory Acts The Church of Jesus Christ was then afraid of offend- ing the manufacturer and the landlord, just as they were afraid of 'offending them now. (Applause.) If the Church was to be "the light of the world " she must bear witness in matters of that sort. (Hear, hear.) She could not avoid it. If she were true to her Lord she was bound to stand up for God and humanity wherever their interests were involved. (Applause.) He did not mean to say that it involved association with any political party, in fact be bad never known any political party worth associating with. (Laughter.) They might be great Imperialists—(laughter)—but there wal no Imperialism so great as the kingdom of God. Proceeding be said " We stand for the establishment of the Kingdom of God. We confront and battle against any force that ruins or debases men and women. If we are to do our work in the world we must bear our witness against these evil things. We must expect to meet with resentment, and we must be prepared to meet it in the name of God and for the sake of Jesus Christ." _ THE DRINK QUESTION. _

He believed that drink was one of the greatest social evils in this country, and was the fruitful cause of a great many evils. The medium by which the Church must act was by the political instrument. It was their duty as Christian people to come apart from our political associations, and never rest until they brought about a, snccessful issue when this country would govern the drink and be no longer governed by it. (Loud applause.) They talked about a German Invasion, did they not realise that England was to-day in the grip of an enemy infinitely greater than Germany could be ? Did they not realise that drink was ruining more than a German campaign would ? There would come a time if we did not realise that intemperance was a foe when the only appropriate home for any Englishman would be a lunatic asylum. We ought to make up our] mindslwe would conquer. (Applause). He believed in patriotism and thanked God for all the great destiny and possibil- ity of the British race and we ought to be thankful for it and we ought all to do all that we could to secure

Is future. (Applause). " Tbe most patriotic institu- tion in this country is supposed to be the House of Lords" he went on. "1 should beliefie in their patriotism if they were more anxious about doing away with the traffic of the drink than about building Dreadnoughts." (Applause). The House of Lords where a number of people met together at the dictates of a financial peer. and they threw out a Bill that meant mo e for humanity than any Bill that had been brought before the country. (Shame). He laughed at their patriotism. (Applause). It was the people of the country that made a nation great and not a number of wealthy persons. They had to ieilise that they could only gAt at of the souls of men as they did away with the obstacles that stood in their path. (Applause).

THE REV. J. E. RATTENBURY.

THE LAND QUESTION.

In London the fact that land was so high priced and rents so terribly high actually caused the damnation of souls. It was a tall statement, but it was none the less true. The people had to live in conditions which stood for their utter demoralisation and the utter demoralisa- eon was brought etout very lirgely by land monoroly. Ho did not think anyone could give evidence that it was an untrue statement. The Church of Christ must do something to stop it. They had to get to the souls of men and between the souls of men there were many obstacles. In his younger days he used to sing "If the Devil is in the way roll over him," and if the House of Lords was in the way roll over them. (Loud laughter and applause.) It seemed to him that we yore boundito be political in our Church activities, and were com- pelled to do political work. If a party stood fur those great matters of moral and social reform in which they were immediately concerned the great majority would feel that those interests were more than the interests for which another party stood. If they tried to escape from it they were blood guilty. If they were ca-eless, indifferent and declined to do their part in saving men and women they were blood guilty. Concluding he said the Church was in the world to save the world and on that great day,fle wotald say "Inasmuch as ye did it not, ye did it not to Me." (Applause.) Dr. Bennett in proposing a vote of Clanks to the Lecturer and Chairman said that although the latter was a member of the Guild they did not see him as often as they would like. However, when he was not with them he was doing good work. He (Dr. Bennett) was delighted to find that Mr. Rattenbury was4to speak on that subject. It had put heart into him tot do more social work, and it had made the members of the Guild far more valiant. The lecture had done much to clear the way for people who were wavering, and now they were bound to do some work for Jesus Christ. Mr. Duckworth seconded and the Rev. Thomas Gladding added a vote of thanks for the inspiting sermon in the afternoon. The motion was heartily carried.
