LOCAL PARLIAMENT. Annual Dinner—ai the Lion Hotel.

The Warrington Parliamentary Debating Society has gained a worthy place • amongst our local institutions and the succesful completion of its third session was signalized by the holding ef the . first annual dinner . at the Lion Hotel on Thursday evening. About 130 members and friends assembled around the festive board and spent a highly enjoyable evening. The speaker (Mr. C. Plinston) presided and amongst others present were :—Mr. W. H. Stansfield (Premier), Mr-. E. Wynne. Jones (Leader of the Opposition), Mr. N. Cook,,jun. (Clerk of the House), Mr. P. Houghton Bennett (hon. treasurer), Mr. Edmund Osborne (hon. secretary), the Revs. Canon Willis, T. W. Barber, Father Wilson and R. E. Letheren, the Maker of Warrington (Alder- man Forshaw), Alderman Bennett, Councillor Single- ton, Captain Crosfield,. Mr. C. E. Parker, Mr. E. J. Hall, Mr. W. Mason, Mr. J. F. Awbery, Mr. J. L. Davenport, Mr. J. Lister. After an excellent repast, served by Mr. John Lee, a lengthy toast list was gone through. The SECRETARY: read letters -of apology from Mr. • A. H. Crostleld, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Revs. E. C. E. Carleton, John Yonge and C. Harvey Cook, Mr. H. Roberts, Mr. W. Peter Rylands, Mr. P. O'Brien, Mr. W. Midden, and Mr. M. G. Hearne (who leaves Warrington next week for Newcastle-on Tyne).

-Mr-. A. 11. Crosfleld, M.P., wrote :—" I regret extremely that it will not be possible for me to be present. May I take this opportunity to offer you most cordial con- gratulations on the great success achieved by our local Parliament, where so many of the debates have been extremely interesting and instructive and so ably conducted."

Mr. W. Madden wrote :—" I take a great interest in debating societies of all kinds, more especially Parlia- mentary ones. I know of no greater schools for the preparation of men young and old for the argumenta- tive battle of life. Ih such schools one learns to have command of one's self and toleration of the just opinions of otherr. They are also a great educational agency. I remember well many years ago when I was a member of a similar institution to your 3 a Cabinet Minister being present in our distinguished stranger's gallery, and afterwards saying that he had that night heard a far finer debit° on the question of the House of Lords than he had ever heard at Westminster."

The toast of "The King" was honoured on the proposition of the Speake-.

/ CAPTAIN CROSFIELD AND OUR DEFENSIVE FORCES

Mr. W. MASON proposed "The Navy, Army, and Reserve Forces."- He said the page of history was studded with the chain cf achieve- ments that had brought honour, credit and renown to the British flag. The glorious record of our n ition was bound up with the efficiency of its troops. Every soldier should be an impregnable fortrp and everyl s tiler a Dreadnought. Until the dawn of that day when universal brotherhood and peace should appear on the horizon it behoved every Britisher to be ready and willing and capable of supporting the traditions of the fatherland. (Applause). Captain G. R. CROSFIELD, in r, sponding, said he was sure his hearers wanted plain speaking and not terminological inexactitudes or naval chronological indetermination. (Laughter). There was one thing that was lacking more or less in the Imperial forces and it was the most important of all—man power which was far more important than chivalry. The personnel of the navy, had sheen cut down by 3;000 men in the last two years and though a great work had been done in building up a naval resarve in the last few years, that reserve was absolutely inade- qute to stand the stress and strain of a great war. Our naval reserve was in the proportion of one to two in the fighting line, but in the case of Germany and France there were two men in reserve fori every men at sea. While other nations were building no arid increasing their staKingth on land our army had been reduced •by ten fine battalions—wiped off, annihilated—the equivalent of 20,000 strong fighting men gone. Apologists would say we had got the Territorials in their places. He was a keen Territorial- ist and thought we Would do the best we could with the admirable organization Mr. Haldane had given us. It was a disgrace to us in Warrington that the local battalion at the present time was 200 men short when every other battalion in the brigade was up to full strength, and it was the duty of every patriotic man in Warrington to do his best by encouraging his young male friends to go into tike ranks to put the battalion into its proper position before the King's visit in July. Captain Crosfield pro- ceeded to argue in favour of national training, remark- ing that what were more serious than the actual want of numbers and training in the imperial forces were the signs of an incipient decadence, the fact that luxury was sapping the moral and physical fibre of the nation. He believed the only check on this luxury was that afforded by the, kindly compulsion of an equal law, making every man and youth in the Empire give some little part of his life to train himself to defend his country. (Applause.) "THE CLERGY."

- Mr. E. J. HALL, in a racy and witty speech, proposed this toast, which he said was a much more militant toast than that which had preceded it. The navy and army were sometimes at peace hut he was afraid the clergy never were. (Laughter). He was not alluding to the little differences they had amongst themselves— (laughter)—but to the constant warfare which it was the duty of their high calling to urge against wrong and sin. "The clergy," continued Mr. Hall, "have a responsibility in the ives of everyone of us, from the

very beginning. They have married many of us— (laughter)—and they look forward to burying everyone _ . . . ___ .

-of us. (Laughter). To them we look, whatever their community, to teach us individually to live lives which shall be true'and sane, so that as a result sanity and truth shall be stamped upon the national character and . . . . .

this nation shall leitd all the nations in the march of progress." (Applause). Tee ll,EcTott in responding, referred to the change which had taken place in the estimation in which the clerical life was held. When he was young the two requisites for a clergyman were that he should be a gentleman and a scholar. While they were very necessary, they were not the only things—and by a gentleman he meant one who knew how to traat the feelings and susceptibilities of others and never to hust

them if he could help it. (Applause). Canon Willis caused great amusement by stating that be regarded Father Wilson and himself as half-brothers, while Mr. Letheren was their brother-in law. The clergy were all opposed to idle less and laziness and they also desired justice and equity, and it was a delight to him to find that in a critical question of that kind which was to the fore at present all the clergy and ministers con- cerned voted on one side. (Applause). The Rev. Father WILSON Slid it seamed to be quite inevitable in a country like ours that there should be religious differences. One of the useful functions of a Debating Society was that it gave a very good oppor- tunity to its members of becoming well acquainted with the views and opinions of all kinds and conditions of men on the most serious and vital questions. The Rev. R. E. LETHREN said he took it thlt in inviting him to speak the members recognised that the interests of religion were supreme and that in compari- son the differences and distinctions that belonged to church order and government were only minor mattersj He candidly confelsed that as far as he was concerned, the particular form in which religion might express itself was a question of far less importance than that the interests of religion should be the supreme thing in our midst. In order to live the highest life of which we were capable we needed the inspiration of religion, and we also needed that inspira- tion to work out worthily the highest ideals for our national life and imperial destiny. (Applause). "WARRINGTON PARLIAMENTARY DEBATING SOCIETY." Mr. A. J. JOLLEY in proposing this toast said the Society had already taken a high and honourable position in the social and intellectual life of the town, and must help to form and mould public opinion. One of the chief aims of the Society was to ensure, as far as it was possible within due rules of order, free discussion, and he took it that all the members bad a determination to accept criticism in the friendliest and kindliest spirit. If till society was to, . .

achieve its highest aims, Criticism, however severe an keen it might be, so long as it was fair and free from personalities, ought to be welcomed. Such criticism led speakers to accuracy of thought and expression and they would also acquire an additional amount of intel- lectual dexterity. Mr. Jolley suggested that through the influence of the society the tone of the oratory of the Town Council might be raised, and its influence might even extend to some pulpits. (Laughter.) Mr. W. H. SfANSFIELD, in responding, said Mr. Grayson had told them that Parliament was a menagerie of wild beasts. That description cauld not be applied to the Parliamentary Debating Society, because the Speaker would prevent them making any such exhibi- tion of themselves. (Laughter.) The Society was a deliberative assembly with educational force and value. It bad come to the ears of the Council of the Society that some people would like to speak but were afraid that their employers would not care for them to do so. He (the speaker) would be sorry if that were the case, and he scarcely believed it could be so in these days, because if what they had to say was true it would come out and was worth hearing, and if it was false it was far better that it should CM() out in their Society than at street corners or be lurking in secret places. (Applause). Mr. E. WYNNE-JONES also replied. He said the study of politics was in harmony with the best intelligence of the ago and he thought the best of the nation's life ought to take part in it. If the best men held aloof from politics the nation would become corrupt and soon go to pieces. Even the churches themselves could not hold aloof from politics. If they did, they would sink to the mere level of spiritual flower gardens and pleasure grounds. They must all work for the time when there would be a Parliament of the world and the federation of men in a common brotherhood. (Applause). Mr. E. OSBORNE added a few remarks. It had, he said, come to his knowledge that some employers in the town had resented certain members taking sides in the debates and some members had refrained from attending on that account. The Society was formed to be a purely educational organization and if members could not attend the meetings and express their feelings and beliefs honestly he feared for the result. There would be an under-current, they would not be • quite as brotherly as they ought to be, and the best interests of the town would not be served in quite as whole-hearted a manner as they would be if members were allowed to express themselves fully. Mr. Osborne paid a tribute to the devoted work of Mr. P. H. Bennett as treasurer and Mr.- N. Cook, jour. as clerk of the House. He hoped the Society would be the means of helping for- ward truth in the town, that the members would band themselves together, and that if any case was brought before their notice where a member of the Society could not come forward and express himself legiti- mately and honestly without fear of losing his -place, the Society would take the matter up, and have it settled once and for all. (Applause). MR. PARKER AND EDUCATION. WHERE WE LAG BEHIND. Mr. C. E. PARKER in/ proposing "The Town and Trade of Warrington " emphasised the important relation which the education and efficency of the rising generation had to the welfare of the town and the prosperity of its trade. He noticed around that bcard a few politicians. (Laughter). Some of them were very much interested in the question of retaliation, preference and Protection, while others were deeply attached to the principles of Free Trade. Those questions only affected directly material things, but.the much more important side was the human factor, the question of the capacity and efficiency of those who would in time have to bear the burden and maintain the prosperity of our town. He could very well conceive a nation hiving all the advantages of Free Trade or, if they liked, of Protection and retaliation, and yet falling behind in the international struggle if the human element was not strong and efficient. There was no central force so powerful and potent and h kving so many possibilities as the human factor. Whatever con- clusion they came to about scientific tariffs,what was of greater importance was the scientific mind, and what- ever their views on Protection, the greater need was that we should pr stect our children against bad environ- ment, against slums in the heart as well as in the street. There was little doubt that wa would have to confess, ant the sooner we acknowledged it the better, that educationally we had slipped behind some of the competing nations' perhaps Germany especially. He had been reading a book by a German in which yarious contrasts with this country were introduced. The writer said : "Anyone starting a new industry in England to-diy will find himself in a tight corner. Materials are plentiful enough and means of transport are sufficient, but the difficulty is to get good technical men." He (air. Parker) did net wish ta imply that everything in Germany was s iperior to the conditions in our own country, but there was no doubt that in the matter of education we had a great deal to learn from ,Germany. The medical inspection of children which was go'ng on in the town revealed if not an alarming at least a serious condition of aft:ars. For example one- fourth of the children had defective teeth and he understood that was one of the main deficiencies which brought about such a large rejection of volunteers for service in the South African war. Ten percent. of the children had poor and defective vision—which might he remedied, if properly attended to, by the use of glasses. Ten per cent. had enlarged tonsils and nearly half of the children w4re irritated by vermin. In the came of the children Germany WM a great deal ahead of us and he ventured to suggest that however much we had to fear from Germany in the matter of Dreadnoughts, we had more to fear from her in regard to her better and higher education. The question of education was one to which every lover of his town and country should •give earnest attention, because it had a very important bearing upon our future prosperity. (Applause). The M&You, in responding, said the Corporation had reduced the rates by 41. in the £ and the price of gas by 2d. per 1,000 and these reductions had taken place without interfering with the efficient working ,of the town's affairs. He-hoped that if the municipal under- takings continued to be successful, there would be a further reduction next year. It was true they had made some little progress with regard to Warrington Bridge, but the width had not yet been decided upon. He hoped they would go in for an 80ft. bridge. (Applause.) Some members of the Council considered GO feet would be adequate but he did not think it would be anything like suffi..ient, considering the great amount of traffic that pissed over the bridge. He was sure that if they took the opinion of the whole of the town, they would find that 81i feet would be the popular width. Warrington could .hold its own with any town in Lancashire with regard to trade and be believed more could be done if the Corporation offered greater facilities to manufacturers. (Applause). The toast of "The Press" was proposed in felicitous terms by Mr. J. F. Avbery. COUNCILLOR FULTON SMITH AND THE WATER SUPPLY. Councillor FULTON SMITH in proposing the toast of "The Visitors" took occasion to call attention to the question of Warrington's water supply, which he con- tended was an urgent one. Even up to ten days ago the supply was short and the day before Good Friday some of the manufacturers had their supply stopped. It was serious matter and ought not to be left in abeyance any longer.

"THE SPEAKER."

The health of "The Speaker " was proposed by Alderman BENNETT who harked back to the days of the old Padgate Wesleyan Debating Society, when Mr. Plinston, Councillor Nield, Mr. H. H. Dutton and he (the speaker) used to join in debate. He voiced the deep sympathy of the members with Councillor Nield in his illness. The society was fortunate in having such a Speaker as Mr. Plinston who was the soul of urbanity but could he stern when occasion demanded. The SPEAKER, in responding, hinted that if he could not participate in the debates in the House 113 would probably le seen taking some definite part in political warfare outside. it was through no ill feeling towards

tal‘ifite his old friends in the Liberal party that he was ge - the attitude he was about to take, but becco,- dlt` thought he was acting on the right lines accorcli" .4e his conscience. (Iv 3 During the evening a high class musical Prel seo, was submitted by Messrs. J. Babbitt. F. Fieldholipt: J. W. Bibby (vocalists), and Mr. E. Balmford Cce„ All the artistes were in their best form and eve'l,-tc" was listened to with keen pleasure. Mr. W. L Mus. Bac., was the accompanist.

The Court will sit on Tuesday, the 27th April, at 11 o'clock a.m., and on every other day during the Assizes at 10.20 o'clock a.m. unless otherwise directed. BAIL CASES will be taken at these Assizes.
